# Identity Governance and License Distribution

## Objective

Develop OpenDirectory into a self-hosted identity and access platform inspired by Microsoft Entra: manage identities, groups, applications, devices, temporary privileges, and software entitlements from one administrative surface. Least privilege is the default across both user and workload identities.

The product direction includes Microsoft 365 and other software licenses through provider-specific connectors. Until a connector is implemented and verified, a local assignment is a record of entitlement, not confirmation that a vendor has provisioned access.

## Repository Baseline

Read-only verification on **2026-10-19 UTC** found upstream `main` at [0ea42c7a53c50462a0de41ab0fd1e2ce24c833ff](https://github.com/Chregu12/OpenDirectory/commit/0ea42c7a53c50462a0de41ab0fd1e2ce24c833ff), using the repository's actual `git ls-remote --symref ... HEAD` result. This checkout is on `codex/entra-app-permissions`, committed base `3d6f468514e231d53df46a76e7f7a4c3cb33112c`. `git merge-base --is-ancestor` returns 0 and `git rev-list --left-right --count HEAD...0ea42c7a53c50462a0de41ab0fd1e2ce24c833ff` returns **253 / 0**: the local committed base already contains the checked upstream main, plus 253 other commits, with no missing commits from that main. It is not an identical main checkout or proof of a deployed release.

The development worktree additionally has substantial uncommitted changes. Test receipts in this document describe those local source snapshots, not a GitHub release. No fetch, pull, merge, branch switch, commit or push was performed for this baseline check; existing work is preserved.

## Design Principles

- Deny actions unless a verified identity has the specific permission for the tenant and target resource.
- Separate authentication, authorization, approval, and actual provisioning. A requested OAuth scope alone is not an administrative grant.
- Delegate tasks to narrow roles; reserving global administration for bootstrap and controlled recovery.
- Resolve the verified issuer/subject pair to an authoritative canonical identity for requester, assigner, and reviewer operations. Preserve the original signed pair as provenance; never trust body-supplied identities.
- Require an independent reviewer where approval is required. Neither the requester nor an individual beneficiary may review that request.
- Make privileges and entitlements revocable, time-bounded where appropriate, and auditable throughout the joiner/mover/leaver lifecycle.
- Report dependency failures rather than claiming successful provisioning using sample data.

## Delivery Stages

### Current Architecture And Boundary Status

| Plane | Actual source and receiving path | Still required |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Human/workload authority | Canonical identity-service links/users/roles feed signed OAuth access, live issuer access/tenant state and the core gateway. The main web consoles use capability-negotiated receivers. | Remaining legacy/signature-only receivers, complete identity lifecycle reconciliation and universal tenant/resource enforcement. |
| Directory administration | Original-actor tenant-owned creation and ordinary reads; scoped user mutations; canonical group CRUD and independent membership reviews with SQL receiving evidence and full group regression acceptance. | OU writes and the role/PIM/review/authentication-policy alternatives described below; platform-wide tenant isolation is not established. |
| Authority migration | Migrations 030/031 prepare exact immutable drafts/source assessments; migration 033 stores independently keyed owned MFA/PIM/review bases in new **sealed** namespaces with mandatory evidence. Migration 034 resolves their exact retained source graph against current SQL evidence without consulting global policy as positive governance. None grant, revoke, publish or activate authority. | Owned live policy/source resolution, publication/adoption, all role/PIM/review command receipts and negotiated consoles. The legacy-source/global-governance gap remains open. |
| Authority target defense | Migration 032 and the actual role/PIM receivers resolve the enabled canonical actor home, filter foreign targets before user locks and bound PIM queues before limits; SQL also fences canonical target/positive PIM lineage writes. | Full owned policy/source and command/receipt cutover. Global policies, imported legacy positive sources, original-bearer post-DML fencing and complete review/policy/UI scope remain unresolved. |
| Local license entitlement | Policy-service owns transactional pools, independent requests/reviews, direct/flat-group source overlap and bounded eligibility/projection; the old license service is an authorized adapter. | Universal tenant/resource scopes, source/event ordering, purchased-pool/billing reconciliation and off-host delivery evidence. |
| Vendor fulfillment | Explicit disabled-by-default Microsoft worker separates local desired state, independently authenticated capacity collection, finite admission and observed vendor state. | Isolated live Microsoft acceptance, production secrets/activation, external lifecycle reconciliation and other vendors. No live license purchase or Graph change is performed by this development run. |
| Gateway/deployment | Production-mode native HTTP exercises normal originless bearer admission and authoritative proxy CORS; all 249 signed regression cases and 120 gateway cases pass. | Actual production Next/container ingress, secret provisioning and operational rollout. Native HTTP evidence does not establish a deployed Entra replacement. |

The following stages remain the platform requirements, not a declaration that every row is already implemented.

| Stage | Work | Acceptance criteria |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Authorization foundation | Inventory administrative routes; enforce service-level permission guards; validate issuer and resource audience; bind actors to verified claims; secure service-to-service calls. | Ordinary users cannot administer identities, privileges, licenses, secrets, or other tenants. Direct service requests receive the same checks as gateway requests. Negative authorization tests gate CI. |
| 2. Identity and applications | Unify identities/groups, app registrations, service principals, consent, scoped app roles, SSO/MFA, and lifecycle workflows. | Roles and consent are assigned only through authorized administration; client-supplied scope requests cannot mint privileges. Offboarding disables sign-in and revokes sessions and app access. |
| 3. Privileged access governance | Wire PIM eligibility/activation into runtime authorization, enforce expiry/revocation, add access reviews and tamper-resistant audit records. | Expired or revoked elevation no longer authorizes requests, including with a previously issued token. Self-approval is rejected. Break-glass operations produce durable evidence. |
| 4. License entitlements | Persistent pools and requests, transactional capacity/duplicate checks, group-based desired-state reconciliation, expiry, offboarding recovery, utilization and renewal reporting. | Concurrent requests cannot overallocate seats; retries do not duplicate consumption. Overlapping groups consume one seat per eligible user. Removing one assignment source preserves access granted by another source. Disabled/deleted users release entitlements according to retention policy. |
| 5. Vendor provisioning | Explicit Microsoft 365/Graph and other vendor connectors; per-provider minimum permissions; secret storage; retry queues and reconciliation. | Show desired versus provisioned state, provider failures and last synchronization time. Failed external provisioning never appears as completed. Validate each connector in an isolated test tenant before enabling it. |

## First Implemented Increment: License Authorization

The existing policy-service API at `/api/licenses` is the source used by `LicenseKioskView`. This increment protects that path without claiming that the whole platform already satisfies the stages above.

### Delegated Roles

| Role | Catalog | Assignments | Requests |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Authenticated user without license permissions | Self-service catalog without costs or notes | No administrative access | Submit for self; read own requests |
| `license-reader` | Read full catalog | Read | Read all requests; no decisions |
| `license-manager` | Read and manage | Read, assign, revoke | Submit for others; read own requests; no review permission |
| `license-approver` | Self-service catalog | No direct assignment | Read all requests and independently approve/deny |
| `license-administrator` | All license operations | All license operations | All license operations, with independent review still enforced |
| Existing `admin` | All license operations | All license operations | All license operations, with independent review still enforced |

Roles come from the verified token's `roles` array or `realm_access.roles`. Individual grants can come from a server-issued `permissions` array. Only these exact permissions are recognized:

```text
licenses.catalog.read
licenses.catalog.manage
licenses.assignments.read
licenses.assignments.manage
licenses.requests.read
licenses.requests.review
licenses.requests.delegate
licenses.subjects.offboard
licenses.subjects.manage
```

`licenses.subjects.offboard` permits local license offboarding only. A verified `users.offboard` permission or `user-lifecycle-operator` role also grants that operation, without catalog, allocation, approval, or reactivation rights. `licenses.subjects.manage` permits reactivation and includes offboarding. Both license administrators and existing administrators receive these rights; `license-manager` does not.

`scope`, `scopes`, wildcard strings, malformed claims, and unknown permissions grant no license administration rights. Role assignment remains the responsibility of the trusted identity provider; this increment does not add a role-assignment editor or turn PIM elevations into those claims. The OAuth issuer now forwards verified user roles/permissions only to applications explicitly authorized to request the `roles` scope. Tokens must contain a nonempty string `sub`. Since the seventh increment, every license operation other than the authenticated kiosk/capability lookup also requires a linked, enabled canonical directory identity, including administrators.

### API Behavior

- `GET /api/licenses/access` returns the canonical `subject` (or null), original `tokenSubject`, `identityState`, effective `permissions`, `demoMode`, and group-reconciliation configuration. The UI uses this response for available controls; the backend independently enforces every operation.
- `GET /api/licenses/kiosk` returns only self-service catalog fields, excluding prices and notes.
- `GET /api/licenses` requires `licenses.catalog.read`; catalog changes require `licenses.catalog.manage`.
- Assignment lists require `licenses.assignments.read`; direct assign/revoke operations require `licenses.assignments.manage`.
- `GET /api/licenses/requests` filters by the authenticated requester unless the caller has `licenses.requests.read`. The optional `status` filter remains supported.
- `POST /api/licenses/:id/request` resolves the verified issuer/subject to a canonical requester ID; the display name comes from verified claims. The default assignee is that canonical user. Requests for another user, group, or device require `licenses.requests.delegate`.
- Approval and denial require `licenses.requests.review`. The requester and an individually targeted beneficiary cannot review the request, including when they hold `admin`.
- `assigned_by` and `decided_by` supplied by callers are ignored in favor of the linked canonical actor. Catalog auto-approval remains an explicit administrator-configured policy; it is distinct from manual review.

The kiosk consumes the real `{ licenses, total }` and `{ requests, total }` envelopes, hides unauthorized controls and private catalog data, and submits self-service requests without a placeholder user identity. It reports the returned request status instead of assuming that auto-approval succeeded.

Next.js forwards `/api/licenses/:path*` to `POLICY_SERVICE_URL` (default `http://policy-service:3004`) before the generic API fallback. Policy-service declares its JWT verification dependency directly. Both Compose variants configure its issuer and internal JWKS endpoint to match their existing identity provider topology. Those providers must issue the server-assigned role/permission claims described above for delegated administration to be available.

### Storage and Demo Mode

License reads and writes require PostgreSQL by default. If storage fails, the API returns `503` rather than silently storing changes in a process-local map or displaying sample licenses as real entitlements.

`LICENSE_DEMO_MODE=true` enables the existing sample-data fallback in development/test environments only. It is disabled when `NODE_ENV=production`, even if explicitly requested. Both Compose variants pass this setting to policy-service; the default is `false`. The kiosk shows a visible demo warning when enabled. Demo fallback is not durable and must not be used for real allocation.

## Second Implemented Increment: Trusted Token Issuance

The OAuth provider now enforces application scope grants before issuing authorization codes, service tokens, device codes, or refreshed access tokens. A request for `oauth.admin` or another administrative scope is rejected unless that exact scope was configured for the application by an authorized administrator. Service-token requests without a scope receive no implicit administrative scope. Interactive delegation of a non-identity scope additionally requires that permission on the verified user or an existing administrator role.

### Authentication and Claims

- Browser sign-in and device approval require a completed authentication-service response: HTTP `200`, `success: true`, and a nonempty verified user ID. They never fall back to accepting a submitted username when authentication is unavailable.
- MFA challenges, failed or malformed authentication responses, and inactive users cannot create authorization codes or approve devices. Optional `mfaCode` fields in both forms are forwarded to authentication-service. Dependency failures return `503`.
- Actor IDs, roles, and permissions come from that verified response; identically named request-body fields cannot override them. Roles/permissions enter access tokens only when the application is registered for and requests `roles`.
- ID tokens have `token_use: id`, preserve a supplied OIDC nonce, and omit administrative role/permission/scope claims. The issuer rejects ID tokens on its management and UserInfo endpoints. Access tokens have `token_use: access` and a unique `jti` so separate issuances can be revoked independently.
- Redirect URIs are revalidated on direct login form posts. Request values are HTML-escaped in browser and device forms. Discovery advertises the implemented authorization-code response and `client_secret_post`/public-client authentication methods.

### Application Binding and Lifecycle

- Authorization codes, refresh tokens, and device codes are bound to their registered client. Another registered application cannot redeem them.
- Refresh tokens rotate on use, have an absolute 30-day lifetime, and can retain or reduce the original scope grant but cannot expand it. Dropping `roles` removes authorization claims from the refreshed access token. Current client scope grants are checked again at redemption/refresh.
- Confidential applications authenticate with their secret, including during device-code issuance and redemption. An administrator can register a public application with `publicClient: true`; public browser flows require S256 PKCE and cannot use client credentials. Public applications can revoke their own tokens without a client secret.
- Client creation, grant updates, and deletion persist before changing the process-local registry. Production rejects client changes without storage and refuses to start without its client database. A nonempty stored registry replaces initial seed clients at startup; malformed stored grants prevent startup. Empty databases retain the existing first-boot client seeding behavior.
- OAuth database connections honor `DATABASE_URL`; authentication follows the existing `AUTH_SERVICE_URL` setting, configured as `http://auth-service:3002` in full Compose. Stored access-token metadata includes the signed expiry and token identifier.

Example metadata for a browser application, submitted by an existing administrator to `POST /api/clients`:

```json
{
  "name": "OpenDirectory Administration",
  "publicClient": true,
  "redirectUris": ["https://directory.example.com/auth/callback"],
  "grantTypes": ["authorization_code", "refresh_token"],
  "scopes": ["openid", "profile", "email", "roles"]
}
```

The returned public client has no secret. Its browser must send a fresh S256 challenge/verifier for each sign-in. The `roles` scope exposes only roles already held by the authenticated user; it does not create a role assignment or administrative consent.

## Third Implemented Increment: Transactional License Lifecycle

### Allocation and Capacity

`LicenseAllocator` executes assignments, request decisions, counters, and their audit entries in one PostgreSQL transaction. It locks the relevant subjects and license pool before allocation so concurrent service instances serialize changes to a pool. Offboarding takes the same subject locks; an allocation cannot race past the disabled marker. SQL triggers also reject overcapacity inserts, shrinking a finite pool below active consumption, and in-place assignment edits.

- Concurrent requests and retries for the same requester/target reuse a pending request or an approved request with an active assignment. Creation returns `201`; reuse returns `200`. Repeated auto-approval consumes no additional seat.
- Approving a pending request for a target that was assigned manually records the decision without consuming another seat. Independent-review checks still apply.
- Expired assignments no longer count toward capacity. Allocation prunes expired rows under the pool lock; a worker also sweeps every 30 seconds. Expiry and its audit record commit together.
- Direct assignment validates target type, target ID, and optional future expiry. Ambiguous revocations require `assignee_type` rather than deleting a user and a device that happen to share an ID.
- The existing convention `total_seats: 0` still means unlimited capacity. Positive totals are finite limits. This is not a provider-confirmed inventory of purchased seats.
- Any failure after a transaction starts rolls back and returns an error, even in demo mode; it cannot silently repeat the operation in memory.

### Offboarding and Reactivation

`POST /api/licenses/users/:id/offboard` requires `licenses.subjects.offboard`. It persistently disables local license eligibility, revokes direct user assignments across pools, cancels pending requests made by or for that user, updates counters, and records audit events atomically. Repetition is safe. The response includes `assignmentsRevoked`, `requestsCancelled`, and `localOnly: true`.

`POST /api/licenses/users/:id/activate` requires the separate `licenses.subjects.manage` permission. It re-enables local eligibility but restores no assignments. A disabled operator cannot reactivate themselves or another account using an old token. Neither endpoint changes the identity provider's account state or a vendor's licenses.

Quick-actions offboarding now requires a verified subject plus `users.offboard` or the existing administrator role. Its first orchestration step calls the canonical policy-service endpoint with the operator's original bearer header; an authorization value in the body is ignored. It does not substitute broader service credentials when delegated authorization fails. An unconfirmed license release is reported as partial completion (`207`, `licensesReleased: false`) rather than successful offboarding.

The policy-service worker consumes `identity.user.deleted`, `user.offboarded`, and `user.offboarding.incomplete` on a durable queue. Delivery is acknowledged only after the local revocation transaction commits; transient transaction failures are requeued. Invalid payloads are rejected without retry. Incomplete-offboarding events allow local release to be retried after a storage outage. These are trusted internal broker events, not an unauthenticated public offboarding API. Publisher availability, broker ACLs, delivery ordering, and a transactional outbox are still outstanding guarantees.

### Audit and Operations

Assignment, revocation, request, decision, cancellation, expiry, and local eligibility changes write `license_audit_events` in the same transaction. `GET /api/licenses/:id/audit` requires `licenses.assignments.read` and returns the latest 200 pool events. Global eligibility events have no pool ID and remain stored in the database. A trigger rejects audit `UPDATE`/`DELETE`; this is not WORM storage, does not withstand database-owner intervention, and does not audit every direct SQL or catalog change.

Migration `005_license_allocation.sql` adds the subject registry, audit table, and capacity/counter guards. Production policy-service now refuses to start unless its database and migrations are ready. Existing overallocated finite pools must be reconciled before this migration can succeed. The kiosk displays cancelled requests as withdrawn rather than denied.

### Verification

- Policy-service authorization, signed-token, demo-fallback, and lifecycle-worker suites exercise positive and negative permissions, forged actors, independent review, commit-before-ack, and transaction failure handling.
- `npm run test:licenses:integration` in `services/core/policy-service` runs 17 tests against real PostgreSQL. They cover parallel allocation, retries, decisions, expiry, rollback on audit failure, offboarding/allocation races, reactivation, capacity changes, raw SQL guards, append-only audit enforcement, and migration reapplication.
- The integration runner uses `LICENSE_TEST_DATABASE_URL` with an isolated temporary schema, or starts and cleans up its own temporary cluster using `PG_BIN`/supported local PostgreSQL installations. Local verification used PostgreSQL 16, without starting the application's database or changing user data.
- Quick-actions tests cover delegated bearer forwarding, permission rejection, confirmed release counts, and partial completion. Frontend tests cover cancelled requests and the existing permission-based kiosk.
- The CI workflow has a dedicated PostgreSQL 16 integration job without `continue-on-error`; the local test runner also includes this suite. Remote CI has not been executed by this change.
- Domain concurrency is tested against real storage; RabbitMQ delivery, the complete deployed service chain, and vendor provisioning are not end-to-end verified here.

## Fourth Implemented Increment: Delegated Quick-Actions Administration

Every registered quick-actions business route now passes through a central default-deny operation registry. An authenticated identity without an operation permission receives `403` before the orchestrator or a downstream call runs. A new path or HTTP method not in the registry is rejected, including for administrators. Only `/health` remains public; the aggregate downstream status report now requires authorization.

### Operation Permissions

| Operation | Required permission |
| --- | --- |
| Read service-principal lists/details | `applications.read` |
| Create service principals | `applications.create` |
| Delete service principals | `applications.delete` |
| Rotate and reveal a new credential | `applications.credentials.rotate` |
| Assign permissions during principal creation | `applications.create` plus `applications.permissions.assign` |
| Single/bulk device enrollment | `devices.enroll` |
| Read enrollment status | `devices.enrollment.read` |
| Unenroll a device | `devices.unenroll` |
| Unenroll with destructive wipe | `devices.unenroll` plus `devices.wipe` |
| Standard user onboarding | `users.onboard` |
| Onboarding with an elevated/admin role | `users.onboard` plus `users.roles.assign` |
| Onboarding into a department outside the configured allowlist | `users.onboard` plus `users.groups.assign` |
| Onboarding with an existing device assignment | `users.onboard` plus `users.devices.assign` |
| User offboarding | `users.offboard` |
| Deploy policies / read deployment status / roll back | `policies.deploy` / `policies.deployments.read` / `policies.rollback` |
| Read the compliance snapshot | `compliance.read` |
| Read aggregate downstream health | `system.status.read` |

Narrow server-assigned roles expand to these grants:

- `application-reader`: application reads only.
- `application-administrator`: application reads, creation, deletion, and credential rotation; no permission assignment.
- `device-enrollment-operator`: enrollment, enrollment-status reads, and unenrollment; no wipe.
- `user-lifecycle-operator`: onboarding and offboarding; no privileged roles, unrestricted department groups, or device assignment. Policy-service recognizes this role for local license offboarding only.
- `policy-deployment-operator`: deployment, deployment-status reads, and rollback.
- `compliance-reader` and `operations-reader`: only the corresponding read operation.

Roles must be arrays in verified `roles` or `realm_access.roles`; individual `permissions` must also be an array. The existing verified `admin` role, including the legacy scalar `role: admin` form in quick-actions, retains all listed operations. OAuth scope text, wildcards, unknown role names, request-body roles, and malformed claims do not grant operations. A stable nonempty `sub` is required. Tokens explicitly marked with a `token_use` other than `access` are rejected. Legacy signed tokens without that marker remain accepted; audience and universal token-purpose enforcement remain incomplete.

### Escalation and Data Boundaries

- Application creation does not authorize consent. A non-administrator with `applications.permissions.assign` may confer only exact permissions already held through verified claims or the delegated role map. Wildcards and malformed grant payloads are rejected before creation starts. New applications have no permissions preselected in the wizard.
- Principal `createdBy` is always replaced with the verified subject before orchestration. Read APIs project allowlisted metadata instead of forwarding arbitrary auth/PIM records, so stored secrets, password hashes, tokens, and nested credential objects are not returned. Creation and authorized rotation can still return the newly generated secret once.
- Onboarding accepts only the actually supported canonical roles `standard`, `elevated`, and `admin`. Elevated/admin roles require independent role-assignment authority; unsupported display labels are not silently persisted as roles.
- `QUICK_ACTIONS_ONBOARDING_DEPARTMENTS` is a comma-separated, administrator-configured allowlist of department groups, defaulting to `General`. Configure it in the quick-actions process environment; `.env.example` documents the setting. Without `users.groups.assign`, an onboarding operator cannot choose another department group. Department names reject DN separators/control characters. This is a workflow allowlist, not a general directory group-delegation engine.
- Wipe must be a real boolean, not a truthy string. Ordinary unenrollment does not imply destructive wipe authority. Existing device assignment in onboarding is also a separate grant.
- The legacy `svc-*` shared credential can still authenticate for migration, but receives no operation permissions; it is not a superuser bypass. Workload callers need properly issued server-side authorization claims. Broad service-token scope strings alone do not satisfy these guards.

`GET /api/quick/access` returns the verified subject, effective operation permissions, onboarding departments, and delegable permissions with `Cache-Control: no-store`. A `null` delegation list denotes an existing global administrator; other lists bound consent to the caller's exact grants, but consent still requires its separate operation permission. The quick-action bar, service-principal list, and creation/onboarding dialogs use this endpoint and fail closed while capabilities are loading or unavailable. The list consumes the real `{ success, data }` envelope. Frontend hiding is advisory; direct HTTP calls face the same independent backend checks.

### Verification and Limits

- Run `npm test` in `services/platform/quick-actions` and `npm test -- --runInBand` in `frontend/web-app`. The signed-token fixture is included in the quick-actions suite; it requires no external identity provider. Policy-service role bridging is also covered by its existing `npm test -- --runInBand` suite.
- The operation registry has table-driven negative, positive, and scope-only tests for all current business route shapes, plus separate escalation and malformed-claim cases. Denied requests assert that no orchestrator/downstream handler ran.
- Existing quick-actions orchestration/E2E suites remain in the gate; status tests now authenticate rather than asserting public infrastructure disclosure. An E2E creation test verifies trusted attribution reaches the downstream auth-service request.
- A separate unmocked verifier fixture starts a local JWKS endpoint and performs 16 checks with real RSA-signed tokens: valid narrow grants, issuer/signature/expiry rejection, ID/refresh purpose rejection, empty subjects, malformed role claims, scope-only denial, realm roles, and the legacy service credential. This verifies the middleware boundary, not a deployed identity provider or complete downstream service authorization.
- Frontend tests exercise capability outages/loading, limited readers, credential operators, bounded consent choices, canonical onboarding roles, approved departments, and disabled privileged/device choices.
- These permissions are currently service-wide, not tenant-/object-scoped. Other services' direct APIs, live PIM grants/revocation, authoritative workload grant issuance, audit evidence for all orchestrated steps, and audience checks still need equivalent enforcement. Securing this orchestration entry point does not secure every downstream route.

## Fifth Implemented Increment: Authoritative Group Membership

Group-based licensing needs a trustworthy membership source before it can safely allocate seats. Identity-service now exposes that source without treating a stale process-local cache as directory truth. This increment does not yet expand a group license into per-user allocations.

### Directory Access and Availability

All `/api` identity-service routes pass through a default-deny operation registry after JWT verification. Exact server-issued permissions distinguish `users.read/create/update/delete`, `users.status.manage`, `groups.read/create/update/delete`, `groups.members.manage`, and `ous.read/create/update/delete`. Search requires both user and group read permissions. Roles include `directory-reader`, `user-administrator`, `user-lifecycle-operator`, `group-administrator`, and `ou-administrator`; an existing `admin` retains all operations. A verified nonempty subject is required, and `identity.admin` scope text no longer grants directory administration. Explicit ID/refresh token purposes are rejected; universal audience enforcement remains outstanding.

User updates accept only profile fields and a boolean `enabled` flag. Changing account status requires `users.update` plus `users.status.manage`; role/permission fields and caller-supplied IDs cannot be smuggled into the directory record. Group metadata updates accept only name/description, not a replacement membership array. Membership changes require their own operation and refer to existing canonical identity users. `DELETE /api/groups/:id/members/:userId` provides persisted, idempotent removal; the existing frontend membership client already uses this path.

Database storage is mandatory by default. Failed reads return `503` instead of cached users/groups/OUs; failed writes do not become process-local successes. `IDENTITY_DEMO_MODE=true` permits explicit development/test demo CRUD only and is ignored in production. Authoritative snapshots never use demo storage. Production startup awaits successful migrations and refuses to listen if storage/schema initialization fails. Health probes check the current database connection and report degraded readiness, including in a storage-less demo. Compose variants pass the demo setting and configure the correct issuer/JWKS topology.

`GET /api/identity/access` reports the verified subject, effective permissions, and demo setting with `Cache-Control: no-store`. It is a capability response, not proof that storage is available.

### Versioned Snapshot Contract

`GET /api/license-groups/:id/snapshot` requires `licenses.groups.read`. `license-group-reader`, `license-manager`, `license-administrator`, and `license-approver` roles grant only this read operation in identity-service, not general user reads or membership mutation. The core gateway and Next.js have explicit routing to identity-service, not the legacy license store.

The database-backed response contains:

```json
{
  "schemaVersion": 1,
  "groupId": "11111111-1111-4111-8111-111111111111",
  "name": "Engineering",
  "revision": "123",
  "deleted": false,
  "memberIds": [],
  "inactiveMemberIds": [],
  "unresolvedMemberIds": []
}
```

- Revisions remain decimal strings so JavaScript cannot round a PostgreSQL `BIGINT`. A sequence-backed revision advances on metadata, actual membership changes, and member enable/disable/deletion. Repeating an already-present membership does not consume another revision.
- A repeatable-read transaction binds the revision and resolved member list to one consistent database snapshot, even if another transaction changes membership between the two queries.
- Only enabled canonical identity-user IDs enter `memberIds`. Disabled users and legacy unresolved member references are separate; a future allocator must reject unresolved input rather than silently inventing identities or treating a partial list as complete.
- A deleted group leaves a durable tombstone with a newer revision, `deleted: true`, and empty membership lists. Its ID cannot be reused. Unknown IDs return `404`; dependency failures return `503` and are not interpreted as deletion.
- Groups larger than 10,000 members return `413`, not an apparently complete truncated list. Responses are marked `no-store`.

Migration `002_group_snapshots.sql` maintains these invariants inside PostgreSQL, including for direct SQL writes: new memberships require existing users, in-place membership rewrites are rejected, deleting users removes their memberships, and status changes advance every affected group. This remains a flat user-membership model, not nested/dynamic group expansion.

### Audit, Events, and Verification

Each identity API write runs in a transaction with the verified subject in transaction-local database context. Group changes, member additions/removals, revision updates, audit entries, and pending group outbox records commit or roll back together. Direct database changes without an actor are attributed to `database`; they cannot impersonate a request body actor. Audit rows reject ordinary `UPDATE`/`DELETE`, but do not withstand database-owner intervention or replace an off-host compliance archive.

The group outbox records `identity.group.changed` with `groupId`, string revision, and deletion state. **A dispatcher is not yet implemented.** Existing live event publishing remains best-effort; a durable pending row is not a claim of delivered events or completed license reconciliation.

- `npm test -- --runInBand` in `services/core/identity-service` covers existing CRUD contracts, delegated/default-deny authorization, storage failure, production/demo separation, migration failure, transaction rollback, snapshot limits, and route contracts.
- `npm run test:groups:integration` runs real PostgreSQL and genuinely signed JWT/JWKS HTTP checks. It verifies concurrent membership inserts, consistent snapshots under concurrent changes, disabled/deleted users, durable deletion, raw-SQL guards, legacy references, audit/outbox rollback, exact actor attribution, migration reapplication, and narrow license-reader boundaries.
- The shared disposable PostgreSQL runner uses `IDENTITY_TEST_DATABASE_URL` or a private temporary local cluster; each suite owns and removes its isolated schema. `LICENSE_TEST_DATABASE_URL` remains supported unchanged for license tests. Neither runner should target production data.
- The CI PostgreSQL job now gates both group snapshots and license allocation; the repository test runner includes both. Local verification uses PostgreSQL 16; remote CI/deployment and RabbitMQ delivery are not claimed.

The sixth increment below connects these snapshots to actual local allocation. Outbox dispatch, vendor provisioning, and complete identity-lifecycle unification remain separate work.

## Sixth Implemented Increment: Group-Based License Allocation

Group assignment now creates a persistent desired-state rule, not a fictitious one-seat group assignment. `license_assignment_sources` records direct and per-group sources independently. `license_assignments` represents the effective per-user allocation: overlapping groups and a direct grant to the same canonical identity consume one seat in total. Device allocations remain separate. Legacy free-form user IDs are not guessed to be equivalent to directory UUIDs; operators must use the canonical directory ID to combine direct and group grants.

### Reconciliation and Capacity

- The allocator fetches the authoritative snapshot from a fixed `IDENTITY_SERVICE_URL`, defaulting to `http://identity-service:3001`, with the original verified operator bearer header. Request-body member lists, identity claims, credentials, URLs, and revisions do not become allocation authority. Redirects are refused, response size/time are bounded, and snapshots are not cached.
- Source arrays, UUIDs, revision strings, deletion state, and unresolved identities are validated before allocation. A persisted per-rule revision/hash fence rejects older snapshots or changed content under the same revision. Viewer-specific identity data is deliberately excluded from that fingerprint.
- Subject locks precede the pool lock. Group changes, sources, effective seats, request allocation state, counters, and audit entries commit together. Offboarding takes the same locks; an in-flight or same-version group reconciliation cannot grant a seat to a locally disabled identity.
- Removal drops only that group's source. Other groups and a direct source retain the seat; removing the final source releases it. Direct revocation likewise removes only the direct source and explicitly reports that scope in the audit.
- If all new eligible members cannot fit, the rule becomes `capacity-blocked`. Confirmed departed members are still removed, existing entitled users retain their sources, and no arbitrary subset of previously unlicensed members is granted seats. Retrying after capacity becomes available converges without duplicating consumption.
- Source expiry is tracked independently. The effective user expiry is the latest remaining finite expiry, or unlimited if any active source is unlimited. Expiry of one source does not remove another. Expired group rules are disabled; extending/re-enabling them requires an explicit authorized configuration change.
- Local offboarding removes direct and group sources. A separately authorized reactivation restores eligibility, not historical direct grants; an enabled group rule can grant access again on its next successful reconciliation.

### API and UI

The existing assignment and request APIs accept a canonical group UUID:

```json
{
  "assignee_type": "group",
  "assignee_id": "11111111-1111-4111-8111-111111111111"
}
```

`POST /api/licenses/:id/assign` still requires `licenses.assignments.manage`. Its group response contains `kind: group`, `localOnly: true`, and a rule; `201` denotes active local allocation and `202` denotes a persisted rule waiting for capacity. Optional future `expires_at` bounds that group source. An omitted expiry preserves an existing rule's limit; an explicit `null` removes it. Neither response confirms vendor provisioning.

- `GET /api/licenses/groups` requires `licenses.assignments.read` and exposes rule state, assigned/desired member counts, last confirmed revision/time, and synchronization errors.
- `POST /api/licenses/:id/groups/:groupId/reconcile` requires `licenses.assignments.manage` and uses a fresh operator-authorized snapshot. It does not re-enable a revoked rule.
- `DELETE /api/licenses/:id/assignments/:groupId?assignee_type=group` revokes only that group source without depending on source-service availability. Explicit target types avoid ambiguous user/device/group IDs.
- Assignment reads include their active `sources`, so administrators can explain retained access instead of assuming a removed group revoked every grant.
- Group requests preserve the existing requester/delegation permissions and deduplicate retries. Approval is a decision; `allocation_status` separately records `active`, `capacity-blocked`, `revoked`, `deleted`, or `expired` state. Capacity shortage never becomes an invented successful allocation.

The license kiosk shows group rules, eligible/assigned counts, errors, last synchronization, and missing automatic-sync configuration. Only assignment managers receive reconcile/revoke controls. Approved-but-blocked requests are labelled as such rather than reporting that licenses were delivered. These are local entitlements; Microsoft 365 and other vendor APIs are not invoked.

### Independent Review Requires an Authoritative Identity Link

OIDC subjects and directory UUIDs are not interchangeable. Comparing those strings would let an approver benefit from a group through a different subject alias. Migration `003_identity_subject_links.sql` adds explicit issuer/subject-to-directory-user links with uniqueness constraints, immutable-update guards, and transactional audit entries.

Only `identities.links.manage` can create/delete bindings; this is granted to the existing global administrator, not license managers or approvers. `POST /api/identity/links` accepts:

```json
{
  "issuer": "https://opendirectory.local",
  "subject": "verified-oidc-subject",
  "userId": "22222222-2222-4222-8222-222222222222"
}
```

The issuer must equal identity-service's configured trusted issuer. Existing bindings cannot be silently overwritten; correction requires explicit `DELETE /api/identity/links` with issuer/subject followed by creation, both audited. Deleting a directory user removes its bindings. Request-body `directory_user_id` or similar claims do not override them.

The snapshot source resolves `viewerUserId` and `viewerEnabled` from its own database using the verified source JWT. Group approval/denial requires a linked enabled reviewer, rejects the requester, and rejects active or inactive group beneficiaries even if their JWT subject differs from the directory UUID. Unmapped reviewers fail closed. A known disabled linked caller also cannot acquire new group grants. Bindings do not yet unify every authentication, vendor, or legacy free-form identity automatically.

### Automatic Operation and Migration

With `LICENSE_GROUP_READ_TOKEN` set, the existing lifecycle worker polls enabled rules every 30 seconds after expiry cleanup. The credential must be a valid server-issued JWT carrying the narrow `license-group-reader` role or `licenses.groups.read` permission; an OAuth scope string or legacy shared service secret is insufficient. Do not use a global administrator credential. Configure/rotate it through the deployment's secret mechanism before expiry; automatic workload-token acquisition/refresh is not yet implemented. Compose passes the fixed source URL and optional credential; the default credential is empty, leaving explicit manual reconciliation available.

Startup registers lifecycle delivery before starting the initial background sweep, so slow group-source calls do not delay API readiness or offboarding subscriptions. Polls do not overlap and cannot re-enable revoked/expired rules. Source errors are visible and grant no new access. Last-confirmed entitlements are retained during a source outage until a successful reconciliation, explicit revocation/offboarding, or their expiry; this is not a guarantee of immediate membership revocation while the source is unavailable. Broker/outbox delivery is still not implemented for group events: polling is the current reconciliation mechanism.

Policy migration `006_group_license_sources.sql` backfills existing direct sources once, merges UUID-casing aliases while auditing normalization, and moves legacy one-seat group records into `pending` rules awaiting a real snapshot. It preserves their intended expiry and does not invent group beneficiaries. Reapplication never converts group-only effective allocations into direct grants. SQL guards reject new fictitious group seats, overcapacity expiry extensions, and arbitrary assignment edits; only source-derived expiry changes are accepted. The current workflow additionally requires identity migrations through `004` and policy migrations through `008`. Back up production data and inspect pending/error rules after upgrade; migration is not external vendor provisioning or revocation.

### Verification

`npm run test:licenses:integration` now runs both direct-allocation and group-allocation suites against disposable PostgreSQL databases. Group tests use the actual identity snapshot store/permission guard over HTTP with genuinely signed operator JWTs, a separate identity schema, and the real policy schema. Cases cover overlapping/direct sources, blocked capacity and recovery, release despite blocked additions, concurrent allocation/offboarding, same-version retries, stale/conflicting/failed sources, tombstones, aliased beneficiaries, unmapped reviewers, independent expiry, delayed worker snapshots, rollback of releases and additions on audit failure, and real legacy migration/reapplication.

Identity integration tests verify trusted binding administration, immutable updates, actor attribution, and rejection of body-supplied identity substitution. API/unit tests verify capability boundaries, original bearer delegation, demo fallback rejection, source input/transport validation, and worker credentials/startup behavior. Frontend tests cover rule controls and approved-versus-allocated status. These suites gate the existing PostgreSQL CI job; no remote CI, deployed broker, external IdP configuration, or vendor tenant is claimed as verified.

## Seventh Implemented Increment: Canonical License Principals

### Authoritative Self-Binding

`GET /api/identity/subject` returns only the caller's verified issuer/subject binding: `{ schemaVersion: 1, issuer, subject, userId, enabled }`. It does not grant directory-list or link-management rights. Missing links return null identity/state; database failures do not masquerade as missing links. Query parameters, display names, roles, scopes, and `directory_user_id` claims cannot choose another identity. This endpoint always reads persistent storage, even in explicit identity demo mode, and sets `Cache-Control: no-store`.

Policy-service calls this fixed endpoint at `IDENTITY_SERVICE_URL` with the original operator bearer token before private license reads and every public mutation. It validates the returned issuer/subject against its own verified JWT, canonical lowercase UUID, schema, and enabled flag. Redirects are refused, response size/time are bounded, and no privileged service credential or token-ID fallback is substituted. Unlinked/disabled operators receive `403`; unavailable or invalid binding sources receive `503`. These checks also apply to explicit development license demo mode.

The authenticated kiosk remains browsable without a link. `/api/licenses/access` reports `active`, `unlinked`, `disabled`, or `unavailable`; non-active states expose no effective license permissions and no canonical subject. The UI avoids private requests in those states, disables ordering/admin controls, and explains the missing link, disablement, or source outage. This state describes directory eligibility; the separate local offboarding marker still independently blocks allocation/decision transactions.

### Identity Bootstrap and Upgrade

An authorized identity administrator must explicitly link the trusted issuer and exact signed `sub` to an existing identity-service user UUID using `POST /api/identity/links`. The link-management permission is `identities.links.manage`; the compatibility `admin` role includes it. This bootstrap endpoint does not require an existing license binding. Its body is:

```json
{
  "issuer": "https://your-configured-issuer.example",
  "subject": "the-exact-signed-token-subject",
  "userId": "11111111-1111-4111-8111-111111111111"
}
```

The issuer must match identity-service's configured `OIDC_ISSUER`; policy-service must verify the same issuer/JWKS. A user UUID looking like a JWT subject is not an implicit link. Links are unique per issuer/subject and issuer/user, audited, and cannot be silently overwritten. Changing a link requires explicit unlink/relink administration. There is no automatic linker or link-management UI yet. Configure a real persistent identity source even when testing license demo fallback; a source outage intentionally blocks privileged use.

New public user-targeted requests, assignments, offboarding, and activation require lowercase directory UUID format. The eighth increment also validates direct beneficiaries against the authoritative directory. Historical noncanonical assignment identifiers are not automatically mapped or relabeled. Their reconciliation, including external provider/lifecycle identifiers, remains an explicit follow-up task.

Migration `007_license_principal_binding.sql` adds trusted request provenance and audit issuer/subject fields. New requests store canonical `requester_id`, `identity_bound=true`, and original `requester_issuer`/`requester_subject`. Private history is filtered by the canonical owner and the bound flag, not a raw token alias. Legacy requests remain administratively visible but are not reused or manually actionable (`409`); recreate them under the bound identity. Migration reapplication never invents provenance for old rows. Historical approved entitlements are not silently revoked by this provenance migration.

Direct and group review require an enabled canonical principal at both HTTP and domain boundaries. Canonical requesters and individual beneficiaries cannot approve or deny their requests, including through different/relinked token aliases and the compatibility `admin` role. Group review also checks snapshot membership and rejects a changed binding between self-lookup and group-lookup. Independent reviewers retain their own signed provenance. Request-local asynchronous context keeps concurrent audit identities separate without storing bearer tokens.

### Verification

- Policy-service: 346 unit/API tests plus 53 real PostgreSQL tests pass. The latter now include the actual policy HTTP app/JWKS verifier, identity snapshot/binding HTTP source, and two independent database schemas, not only mocked SQL calls.
- Identity-service: 91 unit/API tests plus 20 real PostgreSQL/HTTP tests pass, including self-only binding access, link administration, disabled identities, and no guessed subject equivalence.
- Frontend: all 392 tests pass, including unlinked/disabled/unavailable states, capability controls, and non-actionable legacy requests. The TypeScript check still has the same 18 pre-existing diagnostics outside the changed kiosk.
- Signed-token tests cover direct beneficiary/requester alias attacks, explicit non-access token purpose, forged body/token identity fields, source mismatch/outage, demo fail-closed behavior, canonical ownership/offboarding, preserved provenance, concurrent audit isolation, and legacy migration reapplication.

These checks do not establish production deployment, general live-role/PIM revocation, resource audiences, event ordering, cross-provider lifecycle convergence, or Microsoft Graph provisioning.

## Eighth Implemented Increment: Authoritative User Eligibility

### Narrow Versioned Source

`GET /api/license-users/:id/snapshot` returns only `{ schemaVersion: 1, userId, revision, enabled, deleted }`, without usernames, email, or directory listing rights. A caller can read their explicitly linked own user; reading another user requires the verified `licenses.users.read` permission. The `license-manager`, `license-approver`, and `license-administrator` roles include this narrow source permission, not general user administration. A separate `license-eligibility-reader` role grants only that read. Requested scopes and body/token `directory_user_id` fields do not substitute for a binding or a read grant. Gateway and frontend rewrites preserve this authoritative source route.

Identity migration `004_user_eligibility.sql` adds exact BIGINT/string eligibility versions. Insert, enabled-state changes, and deletion create newer versions; metadata changes and repeated identical enabled-state updates do not. Supplied revision numbers cannot overwrite the generated version. User IDs cannot be edited or reused after deletion. Deletion produces a durable disabled tombstone instead of making an unknown `404` look like authoritative offboarding. Audit and pending outbox evidence commit with the user change and associated group revision changes; evidence write failures roll the operation back. Existing users receive a current revision without fabricated historical audit records. The outbox is not yet dispatched.

### Allocation and Recovery

Direct assignment, self-service/delegated requests, and direct review fetch actual user eligibility with the original caller credential before granting a seat. Requests and direct/group reviews also check their canonical requester, not just the beneficiary. A disabled requester cannot obtain approval for an otherwise active beneficiary or group. Unknown users (`404`), source authorization failures (`403`), outages/malformed responses (`503`), and conflicting/stale confirmed versions (`409`) do not grant licenses. There is no broad service-token fallback. User-state bodies are ignored.

Policy migration `008_user_eligibility.sql` records the latest confirmed directory state independently from the local offboarding marker. Subject locks serialize the version fence with allocation. Once disablement/deletion has been confirmed locally, a delayed older positive response cannot regrant a seat, including through an older active group snapshot. Likewise, an older negative response cannot revoke a newer confirmed re-enabled state. Equal-version conflicting contents and deleted-identity resurrection are rejected; ordinary SQL version regression is also guarded.

A confirmed disabled/deleted snapshot atomically removes that user's effective assignments and all direct/group sources across pools, cancels pending requests made by/for the user, updates counters, and records audit evidence. This uses the same subject-first, ordered-pool lock discipline as local offboarding. An attempted request/review can therefore return `403` after authoritative cleanup has cancelled its pending request; the UI reloads failed reviews rather than continuing to show stale approval controls. Audit failure rolls the state fence, releases, and cancellations back together.

Directory re-enablement does not clear local offboarding, restore direct licenses, or reinstate cancelled requests. Explicit local activation additionally requires a currently enabled real directory user. An independently enabled group rule can grant access again only after eligibility is refreshed and the current group is reconciled; manual operators should perform both steps if automatic reconciliation is not configured.

### Operations

- `GET /api/licenses/users` requires `licenses.assignments.read` and returns up to the latest 200 recorded eligibility/error states, including last successful confirmation and last attempt. It does not invent states when storage is unavailable.
- `POST /api/licenses/users/:id/reconcile` requires `licenses.assignments.manage`, fetches the actual source snapshot, and reports local revoked/cancelled counts. It does not reactivate a local offboarding marker or accept state/revision fields from the body.
- The kiosk exposes confirmed enabled/deleted/local-blocked states, versions, timestamps, source errors, and authorized manual reconciliation. Read-only assignment access cannot invoke the reconcile control. Configuration flags indicate that credentials were configured, not proof that a worker is healthy.
- Set `LICENSE_USER_READ_TOKEN` to a genuinely issued, narrowly authorized read JWT for background user reconciliation. Use `license-eligibility-reader` or an exact server-issued `licenses.users.read` permission, not a requested scope. This is separate from `LICENSE_GROUP_READ_TOKEN`; a group-only reader gains no user eligibility read rights. Both Compose variants forward the new variable; the default is empty, so manual reconciliation remains necessary.
- Each lifecycle sweep checks at most 50 candidate users, ordered by oldest/absent attempt for rotation, before group reconciliation. It considers canonical assignment/source/pending-request IDs and previously confirmed nondeleted users. Source credential/outage errors stop the batch instead of flooding the dependency; per-user errors retain last confirmed grants/state. Transport stays bounded to 10 seconds and 2 MiB. Neither automatic JWT renewal nor a strict end-to-end reconciliation latency SLA is implemented.

Directory and policy databases do not share a transaction. A change after the last source read can only be enforced after a subsequent confirmation/poll; there is no instantaneous cross-service revocation guarantee. Outages and unknown legacy identities preserve last confirmed existing local entitlements, but cannot authorize new direct grants. A prolonged-outage access-lease policy remains outstanding. Expired worker tokens require explicit replacement; they never cause administrative credential escalation. Production requires persistent storage; explicit development demo data is not a durable version-fence implementation and cannot bypass failed eligibility reads/negative cleanup failures.

### Verification

- Policy-service: 360 unit/API tests and 66 real PostgreSQL tests pass; identity-service: 91 unit/API tests and 25 real PostgreSQL/HTTP tests pass.
- Frontend: 395 tests pass; API gateway: 162 tests pass. Frontend type-check remains blocked by the same 18 pre-existing diagnostics outside this kiosk. Both Compose configurations validate with disposable required secret values; no deployment is started.
- New coverage includes self-only/minimal source reads, narrow role separation, unknown/disabled/deleted recipients, disabled delegated requesters, tombstones/UUID reuse, stable metadata revisions, upgrade/reapplication, source failures, demo fail-closed behavior, real signed HTTP mutations, stale positive/negative snapshots, concurrent grant/release, rollback after seat release, bounded rotating polling, separate worker credentials, and truthful UI refresh/status controls.

This is local entitlement governance, not Microsoft Graph provisioning, sign-in/session revocation, or a complete Entra replacement.

## Ninth Implemented Increment: One License Authority

### Consolidation Rather Than Parallel Authorization

The separate `license-management` prototype formerly owned process-local assignments, accepted body-supplied actors, exposed unrestricted WebSocket subscriptions containing complete license objects, and subscribed to lifecycle events independently. Its route registrar also referenced absent service methods, so its advertised SAM API was not a reliably initialized implementation. Merely adding another role check would still leave two disagreeing entitlement stores and bypass the canonical identity, capacity, approval, and lifecycle invariants.

The supported `license-management` entry point is now a compatibility adapter to policy-service, not a second allocator. It imports no legacy controllers/services, initializes no license maps, database, broker, mobile sync, remediation, or background assignment jobs, and publishes/consumes no duplicate license lifecycle events. Its dependencies are reduced to the HTTP boundary and pinned by an install-validated lockfile; the container uses the CI runtime major and disables install scripts. Importing the module no longer starts listeners or timers.

Known `/api/license/licenses` catalog/assignment/request paths and explicit request, group, user lifecycle, eligibility, and per-pool audit operations map to their canonical `/api/licenses` equivalents. They require canonical JSON fields and envelopes, not the old prototype's procurement model. Every request uses the original bearer for a fresh policy capability check, then for the actual policy operation, whose authentication, linked principal, permission, ownership, independent-review, directory-state and transactional checks remain authoritative. Exact permissions gate the adapter registry too; header/body roles, scopes, cookies, API keys, actor fields and caller-supplied service credentials never replace the operator. The safe kiosk/access views retain canonical capability behavior. Policy demo mode is not accepted as a persistent authority through this facade.

Transport has a fixed validated service origin, no redirects or caller-provided destinations, no forwarded cookies/service-role headers, a 10-second deadline, a 2-MiB JSON-response limit, and no authorization cache or automatic mutation replay. Unsupported fields, including `licenseKey`, embedded assignments, legacy quantities, vendor/software IDs, terms and unsupported filters, fail explicitly with `422`. They are not silently dropped, converted into unlimited pools, or acknowledged as persisted. Operation outcomes such as `202` group-capacity blockage, `204` source revocation, `403` policy denial and `409` seat conflicts remain canonical.

`GET /api/licenses/:id` now provides an authorized canonical catalog detail read with current effective seat usage; static self-service/request/group/user routes precede it. Ordinary users cannot read private catalog details through either route.

### Retired Surface and Upgrade Requirements

Prototype software/vendor/type catalogs, usage/compliance/optimization jobs, assets/mobile sync, reports/dashboard, legacy renewal/revocation commands and global audit export are not trustworthy alternate licensing features. Their old paths return authenticated `410 Gone`, with a canonical API pointer. Canonical typed DELETE revocation and per-pool audit remain available. No former in-memory or procurement records are migrated automatically; backup and explicitly reconcile IDs, subjects, direct/group sources, seats, contracts and secrets before replacing an existing deployment. This is route-level compatibility with a deliberately changed data contract, not a claim of full old-schema compatibility or complete SAM functionality.

The unrestricted `/ws/license` event stream is retired, not reimplemented with cached legacy data. No handshake or broadcast is accepted. Upgrade authorization uses the same canonical bearer verification, with bounded pending upgrades/deadlines; missing/forged/expired/non-access/unlinked/disabled credentials and unavailable authority fail closed. A verified active caller receives `410`, not an open event channel. Query credentials are not accepted. A future realtime feature must use an independently authorized canonical feed/outbox and per-subscriber projections.

The core gateway now keeps `/api/licenses` on policy-service port 3004 and `/api/license` on adapter port 3018, preserves their full paths, and cannot overwrite its canonical `licenses` registry when the compatibility module is enabled. Source snapshots still go to identity-service. Next.js explicitly routes both prefixes before the generic backend, without legacy fallback. These were routing contracts at this stage; the tenth increment below adds the core gateway authentication boundary and tests the actual chain.

Both root Compose files offer an optional `license-compat` profile on the canonical services' network, loopback-only port 3018, and fixed internal policy/adapter URLs. The profile is unnecessary for the modern kiosk and does not enable the optional core-gateway module by itself. Standalone Compose requires the actual existing network and no longer provisions a second database/Redis or weak-default database password. `/health` is minimal adapter liveness, not confirmation of dependency readiness. No deployment, image build, data migration, broker-feed delivery or vendor provisioning is claimed by configuration validation.

Full path/data contracts and migration warnings are in [the adapter README](../../services/core/license-management/README.md).

### Verification

- Adapter: 32 real HTTP/upgrade/transport tests pass, including every registered operation, permission denial, actor/header stripping, malformed inputs, retired/unknown routes, capacity/operation-time errors, source/demo outages, no cookies/redirects/retries, bounded responses and timeouts.
- Policy-service: 361 unit/API tests plus 74 PostgreSQL tests pass. The actual adapter → policy JWT/JWKS verifier → independent persistent identity source chain verifies shared seats and audit identity, self-service privacy/ownership, independent review, blocked group allocation, recipient eligibility, separate offboarding rights, and real signed/expired/invalid WebSocket upgrade rejection.
- Identity-service: 92 unit/API tests and 25 PostgreSQL/HTTP tests pass; gateway: 163 tests pass; frontend: 396 tests pass. Its type-check still reports the same 18 unrelated pre-existing diagnostics.
- Adapter tests gate both CI/root test orchestration, and its locked dependencies are installed before the cross-service PostgreSQL gate. Both root Compose profiles and standalone Compose validate without starting containers; workflow/module/event-routing YAML parse successfully.

This closes the duplicate legacy runtime path, not the outstanding tenant/resource, live IAM/PIM, session, audience, event-ordering, data-migration or external vendor integration requirements.

## Tenth Implemented Increment: Verified Gateway Control Plane

### One Explicit API Token Contract

The core API gateway no longer trusts a shared `JWT_SECRET`, an environment API key, cookies, query credentials, or a five-minute positive authentication cache. Protected requests require an original header bearer, RS256 signature, configured issuer/JWKS, API audience, required subject/issued-at/expiry claims, and explicit `token_use: access`. Signature and expiry are verified on every request. JWKS public-key caching is not a cached authorization decision. Dependency failures never fall back to another credential or anonymous administration.

OAuth grant access tokens now use `OAUTH_API_AUDIENCE` (default `opendirectory-api`) and retain their issuing application in `client_id`. ID tokens retain their client-specific audience and cannot authorize APIs. Refresh/device-code redemption and revocation remain bound to the original application; token persistence records that application, not the API audience. Introspection does not disclose another client's token merely because both tokens target the same API audience. Request-supplied audience/resource fields cannot choose a different audience. Long-lived legacy enrollment credentials without an OAuth client audience are not upgraded into API-audience tokens; their own legacy device endpoints still require separate resource/purpose/ownership hardening. This is a shared first-party API audience, not per-resource, tenant, or consent-scoped application authorization.

Policy-service and identity-service independently enforce the same API audience, RS256, required claims, and explicit access purpose, including when reached directly. Both Compose variants wire the audience consistently. Lite now includes the same OAuth issuer for these protected paths rather than trusting the old embedded authentication-service issuer. Authentication-service remains the configured credential/MFA verifier; this does not certify its whole deployment or eliminate every legacy authentication route elsewhere.

### Delegated Gateway Administration

| Role | Local gateway rights |
| --- | --- |
| `gateway-reader` | Status, sanitized configuration, webhook metadata/history, event-channel connection |
| `gateway-key-administrator` | Read/deactivate historical key records; no key issuance |
| `gateway-webhook-administrator` | Read/manage webhook metadata and explicitly permitted unsigned test delivery |
| `gateway-administrator` | All local gateway control-plane operations, not downstream global administration |

Exact server-issued `gateway.status.read`, `gateway.config.read/manage`, `gateway.keys.read/manage`, `gateway.webhooks.read/manage`, and `gateway.events.read` permissions are also recognized. Compatibility `admin` remains broad; scopes, body fields, `isAdmin`, and wildcards do not grant these rights. `GET /api/gateway/access` is a no-store capability response for the verified subject. Both key-route aliases, module writes, webhook writes/test/history, detailed health, service inventory, and route information are guarded by an explicit operation registry. Unknown control-plane operations fail closed. Public liveness is minimal; it does not report storage/readiness or expose process/service details.

Identity and canonical/compatibility license prefixes preserve the original token and rely on the receiving service's independent operation/identity guards. Other registered proxies require compatibility `admin` or an explicit `gateway.proxies.<registered-name>.access` claim. Unregistered legacy backend operations default to denial for nonadministrators. These coarse proxy grants are not receiving-service resource authorization; direct legacy services still need equivalent hardening. A gateway administrator is not automatically a device, identity, or license administrator.

The real HTTP integration chain also exposed consumed POST bodies: the gateway's global JSON parser had drained the stream before proxying. Parsing now applies only to owned control-plane routes, with a 256-KiB bound; downstream bodies remain intact. Canonical and compatibility mutations traverse the actual gateway/proxy, not a proxy mock, in the PostgreSQL gate.

### Retired Credentials, Persistent Metadata, and Realtime Limits

Gateway API-key issuance returns authorized `410 Gone`; old/environment keys cannot authenticate. Historical records can be listed without hashes and deactivated only with the proper rights and actual persistent storage. No process-local key or webhook fallback remains. PostgreSQL schema initialization must finish before metadata operations succeed; outages return `503` rather than fake saved records. The integration UI loads capabilities first, hides or disables unauthorized actions, labels old credentials retired, and reports authority/storage failures instead of presenting invented empty success.

Webhook registration is metadata, not proof of event subscription. Signing secrets are rejected because signed dispatch is not implemented. Test delivery is explicitly unsigned, header-token-free, limited to HTTPS origins configured in `GATEWAY_WEBHOOK_ALLOWED_ORIGINS` (empty denies all), rejects redirects, and has a five-second deadline. Delivery responses use actual stored record IDs and counters, including failed responses. External side effects and SQL are not one transaction; storage failure after a send can still mean the receiver saw the unsigned test. No automatic event dispatcher, signing-key vault, retries, DNS/IP pinning, or production-grade outbound firewall is implemented. Do not allow untrusted/private destinations just to make tests succeed.

The core `/ws` upgrade is authenticated and permission-checked before `101`, with bounded pending/concurrent connections and payloads. Credentials are header-only, origins are allowlisted, every message re-verifies its access token, and token expiry closes the channel. Status subscriptions additionally require status permission and a known service. There is no unrestricted raw license broadcast. Browser WebSocket APIs cannot supply this header; a short-lived browser ticket flow and authorized resource event feed are still outstanding. Connecting or acknowledging a subscription does not demonstrate delivered business events.

### Upgrade and Verification

- Reissue existing access/worker tokens with the configured API audience and explicit access purpose. Review every relied-upon proxy permission; previously open/shared-key control routes are deliberately incompatible. Do not restore an HS/key fallback for convenience. This does not migrate old prototype records or provision workload app roles.
- `CORE_GATEWAY_URL` routes Next.js `/api/gateway/*` explicitly to the core gateway before generic backends. Full Compose uses internal `api-gateway:8080` (host port `3016`). Lite's existing platform gateway is a different service and is not hardened by this change: Lite integration administration additionally requires an independently deployed core gateway and a reachable `CORE_GATEWAY_URL`. Until then its capability request fails closed. Do not point it at the legacy platform gateway to bypass that failure.
- Targeted gates: 65 core gateway, 173 issuer, 361 policy, 92 identity, 32 adapter, and 401 frontend unit/API/HTTP tests; 80 license and 25 identity real PostgreSQL tests. The old gateway suite's insecure shared-key/cache/query/fallback expectations were replaced, not preserved as supported behavior. Native upgrade tests cover actual refusal before `101`, scoped connections, invalid subscriptions, ping, and expiry closure.
- Cross-service PostgreSQL coverage includes the actual gateway → adapter/policy verifier → independent identity snapshot source, same-operator audit, durable metadata, denial of body/scope escalation, retired key refusal, unsigned delivery recording, audience/purpose rejection, and canonical allocation concurrency. Issuer persistence/authentication dependencies remain mocked in its unit tests; the native license chain uses real signatures/JWKS and isolated databases, not a deployed full identity provider.
- Both Compose configurations validate with disposable required secret values. No container build, running deployment, real external webhook, vendor provisioning, or production rollout is claimed. Frontend type-check retains the same 18 unrelated pre-existing diagnostics.

At this stage, fresh signature checks did not query live user/session/JTI/PIM state. The eleventh increment below now enforces explicit individual-token revocation in the three protected receiving services, but does not turn role/session changes into universal revocation.

## Eleventh Implemented Increment: Durable Live Token Revocation

### Authoritative Revocation, Not Another Cache

The previous issuer revocation set was process-local, could clear all entries at capacity, and used a fixed TTL even when a credential lived longer. Resource servers did not consult it. An acknowledged revocation could therefore leave an otherwise valid JWT usable through the core gateway or directly at identity/policy endpoints.

Issuer migration `005_token_revocations.sql` now records only credential hashes, owning client IDs, committed revocation time and actual credential expiry in PostgreSQL. Duplicate/concurrent writes cannot shorten a revocation; expired entries are inactive without clearing unrelated entries. Readers share the database rather than an in-process positive cache. There is no Redis/memory fallback in the supported persistent path. Schema failures leave issuer storage unavailable; token issuance checks revocation-schema readiness and does not consume grants while that authority is unavailable.

`POST /oauth/revoke` authenticates the original owning application and determines signed-access-token ownership through actual RSA/issuer verification, including when no process-local access-token record exists. It persists the revocation before acknowledging success or deleting local refresh state. Unknown/nonowned tokens still receive a nondisclosing response without mutating another application's state. Storage failure returns `503`, not a fake successful revocation. Expiry comes from the signed access token or the absolute refresh-record lifetime, not a generic one-hour timeout. The refresh path checks persistent revocation before redemption and rechecks its in-memory credential/client after the asynchronous check, so parallel redemption cannot exploit the new await boundary.

An explicit `OAUTH_REVOCATION_DEMO_MODE=true` exists only for isolated nonproduction development/tests. It expires entries individually, refuses new writes when full instead of erasing valid revocations, and is forbidden at production construction. Compose does not enable it. It is not a substitute for durable authority or a cluster deployment.

### Receiving Services Enforce the Decision

The issuer's custom `GET /oauth/access-state` endpoint verifies the original header access JWT, API audience, issuer, signature, subject, issued-at/expiry and token identifier, then reads the current revocation authority. It is not a general client-secret introspection interface: a caller can check only the bearer it presents, cannot select another subject/token in the query, and receives minimal state with `Cache-Control: no-store`. It accepts neither ID/enrollment credentials nor API keys or URL tokens. Authority outages return `503`; a revoked credential has `active: false`.

After their own signature/audience/purpose checks, the core gateway, identity-service and policy-service now consult this authority on every protected HTTP request. Direct calls cannot bypass the live check. `jti` is mandatory. A single shared transport, `packages/oidc-access-state.js`, forwards only the original authorization header to the fixed configured `OIDC_ACCESS_STATE_URL`, with no redirects, retries, cookies, service-role substitution or positive cache. Responses must match the locally verified issuer/subject/JTI/expiry, be JSON, and stay within 4 KiB and a 2.5-second deadline. Malformed, redirected, oversized, mismatched and unavailable responses deny the operation. Controller/body/request headers never supply the active decision or grant a role.

A committed individual-token revocation therefore denies subsequent HTTP authorization checks, including an administrative license assignment through the actual proxy and directly at policy-service. It does not retroactively cancel an operation whose authorization read happened before the revocation committed; authorization and business SQL across databases are not one transaction. Existing license assignments are not removed merely because an operator token was revoked.

The core WebSocket verifier also uses the live source before upgrade and on every message.
