Idle connections recheck every five seconds, closing with `4401` after revocation and `1013` when authority is unavailable. Normal source/transport bounds add to that polling interval; this is not instantaneous push revocation. Concurrent connections/upgrades remain bounded. No business-event feed is introduced. Gateway HTTP ingress receives a pre-authentication rate limit too. The issuer separates its access-state read budget from token/login routes, so idle checks do not consume the latter's quota; overload still fails closed and requires capacity/availability monitoring.

### Honest Logout and Rollout Limits

The old `/oauth/logout` did not revoke anything and redirected to an arbitrary query URL. It now returns explicit `410 Gone` without redirecting and is no longer advertised as an OIDC end-session endpoint. This is retirement of a false capability, not a whole-session logout implementation. Applications must use actual client-authenticated revocation rather than interpreting a redirect/local token deletion as invalidation elsewhere.

**This increment revokes individual credentials, not whole sessions.** Revoking an access token does not revoke its refresh credential or sibling access tokens. Revoking a refresh credential prevents its redemption but does not invalidate already-issued access tokens. Refresh grants, authorization/device codes and user authorization snapshots remain process-local, and a renewed token may retain old roles until a true session/identity authority is implemented. Automatic disable/delete/role/PIM propagation, refresh-family reuse detection, session-wide/administrator revocation, browser logout and the corresponding UI remain outstanding. Existing authentication-service sessions and legacy internal-service header bypasses are separate, not consolidated by this registry.

Every other resource service still needs the same receiving-side enforcement; putting a call through this gateway does not harden a directly reachable legacy service. Volatile signing keys remain an independent production/replica blocker: database revocation persistence is verified across store instances, not a claim that the current issuer can safely operate multiple signing replicas or preserve tokens across restarts. Authenticated internal transport, per-resource/tenant scope, durable issuer/client/refresh state and migration from old revocation state still require an explicit rollout design.

Both Compose configurations explicitly wire the live authority for protected identity/license services; full Compose does so for the core gateway too. The four affected core images now build from repository root so shared verifier/event-bus sources are included, and issuer migrations are actually copied into its image. Build manually with `docker build -f services/core/<service>/Dockerfile .`, not the former service-only context. Root `.dockerignore` excludes local dependencies, build outputs, `.env` files and VCS/agent metadata. This packaging/configuration work is not a verified image build or deployment; runtime/dependency support, durable keys, full authentication-service topology and production secrets still need validation. Existing expired revocation rows are ignored but no automatic database-retention worker is implemented.

### Verification

- `npm run test:revocations:integration` in oauth-provider is a new CI/root gate using a disposable real PostgreSQL cluster/schemas. Ten tests exercise actual issuer-signed tokens, JWKS, gateway, direct identity/policy services, canonical identity links and license mutations, with no SQL or proxy mocks. The upstream credential/MFA service is an isolated HTTP fixture, not a deployed authentication-service.
- Coverage includes committed live revocation, unchanged license seat counts after denied mutations, actual expiry/idempotency/concurrent writes, migration reapplication, independent store readers, ownership isolation, credential-purpose/URL/header rejection, refresh denial without fake family semantics, storage-loss denial for reads/issuance/writes, and idle WebSocket revocation/authority-loss closure.
- Shared-client tests cover fixed routing, minimal header forwarding, no cache/retry/redirect fallback, strict response binding, malformed/oversized bodies and transport timeout. Unit tests also guard production demo refusal, bounded demo behavior, storage-failure handling, failed-migration readiness and concurrent refresh redemption.
- Current targeted gates: 180 issuer, 90 core gateway, 361 policy, 92 identity, 32 adapter, 401 frontend tests; 80 license, 25 identity and 10 new revocation PostgreSQL tests. Both Compose configurations validate without starting containers. Frontend type-check still reports the same 18 unrelated diagnostics; no new frontend source is introduced in this increment.

The next increment below adds durable refresh families to this enforcement. Authoritative user/role propagation remains separate; individual-token revocation alone was not completed sign-out or account disablement.

## Twelfth Implemented Increment: Persistent Session Families

### Durable Refresh and Family-Wide Enforcement

OAuth migration `006_sessions.sql` adds persistent sessions, hashed refresh credentials and append-only session audit records. A session binds the exact issuer, subject, principal type (`user` or `application`), issuing client, original authentication time and absolute expiry. User and workload namespaces cannot impersonate one another just because their identifiers match. SQL guards prevent identity/lifetime changes, reactivation of revoked sessions, resetting consumed refresh credentials, or ordinary audit modification. The audit is local database evidence, not off-host WORM storage.

Authorization-code grants now create a persisted family before returning tokens. Refresh credentials are emitted only when the client has the refresh grant; their maximum lifetime is thirty days from the original authentication, not another thirty days after each renewal. Client-credentials and device-code access grants also receive persistent sessions but do not automatically gain refresh tokens. Access/ID tokens include `sid`, `auth_time` and the explicit actor type. The issuer no longer uses the old process-local access/refresh token map as authorization state. Optional legacy access-token metadata is not that authority; the misleading in-memory active-token gauge is replaced by a counter of signed tokens, including uncommitted signing attempts.

Refresh redemption locks the credential and session, verifies client binding before replay detection, checks absolute lifetime/revocation and current client scope grants, and commits old-token consumption, a hashed replacement and audit together. Scope can only narrow; dropped authorization claims are not restored. An owning-client replay of a consumed credential commits family revocation before returning `invalid_grant`, invalidating the winning refresh token and sibling access tokens too. Another client cannot trigger this revocation. Concurrent legitimate refreshes can therefore force reauthentication: clients must serialize refresh across their callers/tabs, not automatically retry a consumed token. The rotation/replay design follows [RFC 9700 section 4.14](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html#section-4.14), not a claim of complete OAuth production compliance or sender-constrained credentials.

Creation, rotation, single-family and bulk revocation are transactional with their audit writes. Audit failure returns `503` and rolls back credential consumption/revocation. Bulk revocation uses one audited SQL operation rather than an unbounded application loop. Mutating transactions have bounded SQL/lock waits; overload is a failure, not a partial successful sign-out. Only hashes of opaque refresh credentials are stored. Profile/authorization snapshots remain sensitive database data and still need a production retention/encryption policy.

### Narrow Session Control API

All routes below require an original header bearer, current session/token state, and `Cache-Control: no-store`. They accept neither cookies, query credentials/selectors nor body-supplied actors or permissions.

| Route | Authorization and behavior |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /oauth/sessions` | Approved `sessions.self.read` scope; latest 100 own session summaries only, including current/expiry/revocation state, no credentials, claims snapshot or other-user inventory. |
| `DELETE /oauth/sessions/:id` | Approved `sessions.self.revoke` scope for an owned family; nonowned IDs are hidden with `404`. Narrow server-issued session administration may target a different family in the same issuer. |
| `POST /oauth/sessions/current/revoke` | Approved `sessions.self.revoke`; terminates the family identified by the bearer itself, including sibling access tokens and all refresh descendants. |
| `POST /oauth/sessions/revoke-all` | Approved `sessions.self.revoke`; terminates the caller's currently persisted active families, not another body-selected user. |
| `POST /oauth/users/:subject/sessions/revoke` | User principal with exact server-issued `sessions.revoke`, `session-administrator` or compatibility `admin`; targets that issuer-specific user subject only. This is not a guessed canonical-directory UUID lookup. |

Self-service scopes must be explicitly approved for the application. They do not grant global session administration. The seeded internal portal supports those scopes; existing persisted clients are not silently upgraded and require authorized grant changes. The narrow administrator role cannot administer OAuth clients or read the general identity directory just because it can terminate sessions. Application principals remain separate and cannot use a user session owner namespace. Global session inventory, pagination beyond the latest summaries, a session-management UI and independent review of sensitive global sign-out are not implemented.

The existing live `access-state` authority now also verifies persisted family state and the issuer/client/subject/type/authentication binding for first-party API tokens. Revoking a family therefore denies sibling access through the gateway and directly at identity/policy endpoints, including license administration; idle native WebSockets close through their existing bounded rechecks. Individual **access-token** revocation remains individual. Revoking a recognized **refresh credential** through `/oauth/revoke` now deliberately terminates its whole family, including access tokens; this strengthens the previous increment's refresh-only behavior.

### Restart Evidence and Explicit Limits

The PostgreSQL gate starts a second, fresh issuer HTTP process and redeems an existing refresh credential there, then continues its replacement in the original process. No token-map state is transferred. This proves durable refresh/family state, not a full clustered identity-provider deployment. Signing keys remain ephemeral. Key IDs now derive from the actual public key instead of reusing one constant ID for different keys, but this does not provide durable keys, rollover overlap or uninterrupted validity of pre-restart access tokens. Stable protected signing material and safe multi-instance client/issuer state are still production prerequisites.

Whole-family termination is now a real header-authenticated API operation. The retired browser GET `/oauth/logout` still returns `410` without arbitrary redirects and is not advertised as an OIDC end-session endpoint; its error describes the missing browser protocol and points to session revocation. Browser/RP logout, front-/back-channel logout, authentication-service's separate sessions and the web application's older client/endpoint assumptions are not unified. No newly working end-to-end browser login/session UI is claimed.

Session revocation is not account disablement. It does not block a fresh credential-verified login or an authentication grant that has not yet created a family. Bulk sign-out operates on current persisted families at its database selection point. In-flight API authorization/operations are not retroactively cancelled. Live directory disable/delete, role/PIM changes, password-reset propagation and a user authorization generation fence still need authoritative cross-service integration. Identity binding and license-recipient eligibility remain separate existing safeguards.

Refresh claims are still verified-at-login snapshots, not live authorization leases. Authorization/device codes remain process-local; long-lived signing/client/application authorization and tenant/resource boundaries remain incomplete. A refresh family cannot be moved to another issuer/subject/client by changing stored fields. Source outages deny access or mutation rather than creating fallback sessions. Expired/consumed records currently have no automatic retention worker; consumed credentials must not be discarded early because they carry reuse evidence. Production load, recovery, encryption and retention remain outstanding.

### Upgrade and Verification

- Reissue access/worker tokens and reauthenticate old refresh sessions: unregistered legacy API tokens do not gain fabricated session rows. Migrations do not guess authentication-service/directory ID mappings or migrate process-local refresh secrets. Update application grants for self-service scopes explicitly; do not restore a no-session or memory fallback to make old tokens work.
- The explicitly nonproduction `OAUTH_REVOCATION_DEMO_MODE` now also selects bounded, transactional in-memory session modeling; production construction refuses it. It is not a shared or durable deployment mode.
- The existing CI/root `test:revocations:integration` gate expands to 21 real PostgreSQL/HTTP tests, including persisted refresh across a fresh process, sibling/family revocation at actual receiving services, owner/type/client isolation, scope and narrow-role denial, parallel refresh/replay, atomic audit rollback, bulk revocation, SQL fences, migration reapplication, session-source failure and already-open family WebSocket closure. Credential/MFA verification remains an isolated upstream HTTP fixture, not a deployed authentication-service.
- Current targeted gates: 188 issuer, 90 gateway, 361 policy, 92 identity, 32 adapter, 401 frontend tests; 80 license, 25 identity and 21 token/session PostgreSQL tests. Both Compose configurations validate without starting containers. The same 18 unrelated frontend TypeScript diagnostics remain. No image build, production deployment, browser end-session protocol or vendor license provisioning is claimed.

## Thirteenth Implemented Increment: Protected Persistent Signing Keys

### Explicit Key Authority

The issuer no longer generates a new OAuth signing key on every normal boot. `OAUTH_SIGNING_KEY_FILE` selects a pre-provisioned, absolute-path, unencrypted PEM RSA private key. Missing, unreadable, malformed, bundled, oversized, weak or incorrectly permissioned material stops initialization before serving requests. There is no downgrade to a generated key when a configured file is invalid. Ephemeral keys require explicit `OAUTH_EPHEMERAL_SIGNING_KEYS=true` in nonproduction only; production refuses that flag even if a valid file was also supplied.

The loader opens the actual file descriptor, requires a regular file, caps input at 64 KiB, and checks POSIX ownership/modes. Private files must be owned by the service user or root and have no group/other permissions; OS readability must also permit the service to open them. Use `0400`/`0600` under the intended service UID, not `0644` to work around a container ownership problem. Trusted public JWKS files may be publicly readable but not group/other writable. Protect parent directories, ACLs, backups and secret-manager access separately: these mode checks are not ACL auditing, encrypted-at-rest storage, KMS/HSM custody or Windows ACL support.

Accepted material is RSA 2048–8192 bits with exponent 65537; the offline generator defaults to 3072-bit PKCS8. Active key IDs use the SHA-256 RSA public [JWK thumbprint format from RFC 7638](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc7638), independent of PEM encoding or process lifetime. No private parameters are published, serialized as metadata or logged by the helper. Token signing still uses RS256; a known key ID cannot substitute another signature or an HMAC key.

`OAUTH_VERIFICATION_JWKS_FILE` optionally supplies explicitly trusted public-only verification material for overlap. It accepts a small, bounded RSA verification inventory, preserves approved legacy key IDs, deduplicates identical entries, and rejects ambiguous IDs, private parameters, weak keys, incompatible algorithms/purposes and remote key references. The active public key is always included; at most eight unique IDs are published. JWT headers select only from this fixed local inventory, never a caller-supplied URL/JWK. Issuer access-state, userinfo, introspection, session control, token revocation and local admin verification now share the configured public trust set instead of assuming only the active key can verify a token.

Keys are an immutable startup snapshot. Editing or replacing a file alone does not rotate an already running process. Restart/reload through a reviewed deployment, and coordinate all issuer instances. Do not leave an old signing key trusted indefinitely just because its public half is not secret.

### Provisioning Without Silent Replacement

The helper is available in the checkout and issuer image:

```sh
mkdir -p secrets/oauth-signing
chmod 700 secrets/oauth-signing
(
  set -e
  set -o noclobber
  node services/core/oauth-provider/scripts/signing-keys.js generate \
    "$PWD/secrets/oauth-signing/active.pem" \
    > "$PWD/secrets/oauth-signing/verification.jwks.json"
  chmod 644 secrets/oauth-signing/verification.jwks.json
)
```

Run with installed issuer dependencies, as the intended local service/secret owner. The parent must already be private and owned by that user. Creation is exclusive, writes a `0600` private file, and synchronizes it to the filesystem; an existing key is never overwritten. Stdout contains only the public JWKS. Shell `noclobber` also prevents redirection from replacing an existing file. Do not run generation against an existing key; `export-public /absolute/path/to/key.pem` instead re-exports that key's public metadata to a new/reviewed destination. Never redirect public output over the private key. No real deployment keys were generated in this development increment; tests use owned disposable directories.

For native Node execution, set `OAUTH_SIGNING_KEY_FILE` and, when needed, `OAUTH_VERIFICATION_JWKS_FILE` to absolute paths. The example environment distinguishes those runtime paths from Compose's host paths. Both root Compose variants bind only the issuer's active private file and public manifest read-only, refuse automatic missing-host-path creation, and explicitly disable ephemeral keys. The initial manifest may contain the active public key (deduplicated) or an empty `keys` array.

Compose defaults to nonroot UID/GID `1000:1000`; provision files readable under that identity. For local development with operator-owned files, set `OAUTH_SERVICE_UID`/`OAUTH_SERVICE_GID` to the appropriate nonroot host identity, rather than weakening private permissions. Production should use a dedicated service identity and protected persistent/secret-manager storage. Bind-mount UID/mode behavior must be checked on the target host. Private material is not mounted into downstream services. Root build contexts exclude secret directories and common private-key formats; the image includes the helper, not generated keys. Existing Helm manifests are not yet wired to these protected file/ownership requirements and need migration before this image can serve there. Configuration validation here is not a chart/container deployment test.

### Planned Rotation and Emergency Removal

1. Generate the next private key separately; preserve the existing key and the authenticated public inventory. Publish its **public** key alongside the active key before changing the signer, and allow for configured verifier cache/cooldown propagation.
2. Switch the issuer to the next private file while retaining the previous public key/ID. Roll coordinated issuer instances with that overlapping trust set. Existing access signatures, current session bindings and rotating refresh families continue without copying private material into receivers.
3. Remove retired public IDs after the applicable token horizon, or deliberately sooner for compromise response. All live authority instances must use the pruned set. A stale receiver JWKS cache does not override the fresh issuer state check at the three hardened receivers; it is not a guarantee for legacy services that still omit that check.

The PostgreSQL gate proves both cross-process continuity with the same protected private key and overlap with different signers. Old/new access tokens traverse the actual gateway, direct identity/policy services and issuer management paths; revoking an old-key token through the new signer still works. A separate gateway deliberately retains the old public JWKS while consulting a pruned authority, and denies the removed-key token despite successful local signature verification.

Key removal does not itself revoke a persisted session or an otherwise valid refresh credential; incident response may require both key and family/user action. Likewise, a retained public key remains signing authority for that key until removed. Existing legacy device enrollment credentials use the OAuth signer and may have much longer lifetimes than ordinary access tokens; do not assume every affected credential lasts only one hour. Device credential lifecycle separation remains outstanding. SAML still has a separate ephemeral prototype signer; this increment does not certify SAML signing/certificates, browser SSO or complete identity-provider HA.

For upgrade from an ephemeral issuer, approved public metadata can be captured before stopping it and explicitly retained while the new protected key becomes active. Private-key recovery is not fabricated. Legacy credentials without the current persisted session binding still require reauthentication; rotation cannot manufacture that provenance. Client-registry coherence, local authorization/device codes, authoritative role/user generations, browser login/logout integration, recovery/retention, hardware key custody and full production rollout remain separate work.

### Verification

- Issuer: 216 unit/API tests, including real file modes, format/size/algorithm constraints, thumbprint equivalence, nonproduction-only opt-in, public-only overlap, malicious header/config rejection, exclusive CLI creation/export and real production initialization refusal with a missing key.
- The existing PostgreSQL gate expands to 23 tests using actual protected files, shared sessions, a fresh issuer process, two signing identities, overlap/removal, real receiving services and cached-public-key versus live-authority behavior. Credential/MFA verification remains an upstream HTTP fixture, not a deployed authentication-service.
- Other targeted gates: 90 gateway, 361 policy, 92 identity, 32 adapter and 401 frontend tests; 80 license and 25 identity PostgreSQL tests. Both Compose files validate with disposable required secret values without provisioning files or starting containers. The same 18 unrelated TypeScript diagnostics remain.

## Fourteenth Implemented Increment: Confidential Browser Token Mediation

### Browser Trust Boundary

The web application no longer treats an `auth_user` object or an access/refresh token in localStorage as authentication. Those obsolete values are removed, not migrated or decoded into a fabricated administrator. The guarded page waits for the same-origin server session response before mounting administrative views. Verified user claims come from the issuer's signed access token, not a caller-supplied role, profile object or requested OAuth scope.

The architecture is a **confidential token-mediating backend**, not a full API-proxy BFF. This distinction follows [RFC 10017, section 6.2](https://www.ietf.org/ietf-ftp/rfc/rfc10017.html#section-6.2): the backend performs code exchange and retains refresh credentials outside JavaScript access, while short-lived access tokens enter frontend memory. Consequently XSS can still obtain/use access tokens or call mediation APIs through the browser. This increment does not claim complete RFC conformance, sender-constrained tokens or XSS immunity.

- `GET /api/auth/oidc/login` generates fresh server-side state, nonce and S256 verifier. An authenticated-encrypted, expiring HttpOnly flow cookie binds the callback. Authorization uses the canonical `/oauth/authorize`; browser code never receives the confidential-client secret or verifier.
- `GET /auth/callback` requires a unique matching state/code and clears the flow cookie. Code exchange is server-side and bound to the exact configured callback/verifier. ID/access tokens must pass RS256 verification, pinned internal JWKS, exact issuer, their separate client/API audiences, purpose, user actor, client/family/authentication binding and nonce checks. A fresh authority check is required before establishing the browser session. Redirects are fixed to `/dashboard`, not caller-provided destinations.
- The refresh credential, subject, family, authentication time, CSRF value and absolute expiry are sealed with AES-256-GCM under a separate 32-byte browser key, bound to origin/issuer/client/purpose. Refresh credentials and identity tokens are not returned in session JSON or placed in browser-readable storage. This is client-side encrypted session storage, not a server-side browser-session database.
- `POST /api/auth/oidc/session` and `/logout` require the exact configured Origin and CSRF value; foreign Fetch Metadata is refused. HTTPS session cookies are `__Secure-`, HttpOnly, SameSite Strict and limited to `/api/auth/oidc`; the readable CSRF cookie is `__Host-`, Secure and host-only at `/`. The callback flow is SameSite Lax to permit the authorization redirect. All responses are no-store. Native HTTP is accepted only by explicit loopback development opt-in, not for arbitrary hosts.
- JavaScript retains only the access token/profile in module memory. Session bootstrap, heartbeat, refresh and logout use a single-flight promise plus an origin-scoped [Web Lock](https://w3c.github.io/web-locks/) to serialize cookie-consuming operations across tabs. Missing Web Locks fail closed. A still-live, correctly bound in-memory access token avoids unnecessary refresh rotation; its live state is still checked. Rotation cookies are retained on subsequent authority/JWKS outages so a retry does not deliberately replay the spent credential.
- API clients use same-origin routes and the original user bearer. Quick-action POST/DELETE operations are never automatically replayed on 401; a resource denial is not automatically treated as global session loss. The page rechecks on visibility and every 30 seconds, hides its views on session-source errors and redirects to login on confirmed 401. Receiving-service authorization remains mandatory regardless of visible controls.
- Browser logout revokes the cookie-bound refresh family through the authenticated issuer and clears cookies only after confirmation. An outage returns 503 and a visible failure, not fabricated sign-out. This replaces the old web client's fake end-session redirect; the issuer's retired `/oauth/logout` remains 410. Other applications' sessions, federated front-/back-channel logout and an owner/admin browser session-management screen are not implemented.

### Explicit Client and Secret Provisioning

There is no automatic weak/public `web-app` registration. Configure `OAUTH_WEB_CLIENT_SECRET_FILE` plus an explicit JSON `OAUTH_WEB_REDIRECT_URIS` list; the optional ID defaults to `web-app`. Allowed callbacks are exact HTTPS `/auth/callback` URIs without query/fragment/credentials. Initial empty-registry provisioning seeds only code/refresh grants and `openid profile email roles sessions.self.read sessions.self.revoke` scopes. It does not grant client credentials, application administration or license administration. Login roles still require the verified upstream user's authorization.

An existing nonempty client registry must already contain the matching approved confidential client. Startup checks its ID, secret, grants, scopes and callback set, failing on differences rather than silently restoring deleted clients or overwriting authorized grant changes. For an existing deployment, register/reconcile through the current authorized issuer client API first; securely provision its returned secret and use its actual ID in both services. Do not delete the client registry to bypass this check. At this increment, protected frontend files do not solve client records/cache coordination or plaintext legacy client-secret storage in PostgreSQL; increment twenty below replaces that registry and storage contract.

For a new local setup, create protected files explicitly, after installing dependencies:

```sh
mkdir -p secrets/browser-auth secrets/oauth-signing
chmod 700 secrets/browser-auth secrets/oauth-signing
node frontend/web-app/scripts/browser-secret.cjs cookie "$PWD/secrets/browser-auth/cookie.key"
node frontend/web-app/scripts/browser-secret.cjs client "$PWD/secrets/browser-auth/client.secret"
```

The helper exclusively creates 0600 files in an existing owned 0700 directory, synchronizes writes and never prints secret material or overwrites existing files. Provision a separate identical copy of the approved client secret for the issuer, at `secrets/oauth-signing/web-client.secret` by default; use protected, non-overwriting secret-management tooling. Do not generate a second, different client secret. The browser cookie key is **not** the issuer RSA signing key, and is never mounted into the issuer. No real deployment secrets were generated by the agent.

Both Compose variants mount these secrets read-only, with missing-host-path creation disabled. Issuer defaults to UID/GID 1000; frontend defaults to 1001. Each copy must be OS-readable by its own service identity with no group/other permission bits. For local operator-owned files, explicitly configure both service UID/GID pairs; production needs dedicated identities and protected ownership. Do not use 0644 to share one private file between different UIDs. POSIX checks do not audit ACLs, secret-manager policy or backup encryption.

Native frontend variables are `BROWSER_AUTH_ORIGIN`, `BROWSER_AUTH_ISSUER`, `BROWSER_AUTH_PUBLIC_URL`, `BROWSER_AUTH_INTERNAL_URL`, `BROWSER_AUTH_CLIENT_ID`, `BROWSER_AUTH_API_AUDIENCE`, `BROWSER_AUTH_KEY_FILE` and `BROWSER_AUTH_CLIENT_SECRET_FILE`. They are server-only. Compose maps public origin/issuer, internal `http://oauth-provider:3010`, API audience and the protected files; host-path variables are separate. Align the callback allowlist with the application origin. An operator must route the public issuer URL to the actual OAuth issuer, not the older authentication-service. The unused legacy `/oidc/*` rewrite was removed. Existing Helm wiring, external TLS/reverse proxy, deployment readiness and all legacy downstream protocols still need target-specific work.

Frontend replicas must share the intended cookie key/client configuration; issuer replicas must share the reviewed signing/trust/client/session state. Changing the cookie key invalidates browser cookies but does not automatically revoke every persisted family. Lost responses, tab/process crashes or manual uncoordinated refresh calls can still cause refresh-reuse detection and require reauthentication; Web Locks are cooperative browser coordination, not a distributed server transaction or grace-period bypass. Frozen tabs may wait/timeout rather than perform unsafe parallel refresh. Stolen cookies remain credentials; protect TLS, logs, host/subdomain ownership, CSP and deployment secrets separately.

### Verification and Unfinished Scope

- `npm run test:browser-auth --prefix frontend/web-app`: 34 native HTTP/crypto tests cover secret permissions/nonreplacement, sealed cookie purpose/origin/expiry/tampering, S256/state/nonce, signature/issuer/audience/purpose/actor/family binding, CSRF, live denial, JWKS/source outages, refresh-cookie preservation and confirmed logout.
- `npm run test:browser:integration --prefix services/core/oauth-provider`: four tests launch actual headless Chrome, Next.js routes, a normally started issuer with persisted client registration, protected signing/secret files and an isolated PostgreSQL schema. They verify browser login, nonadmin management denial, no browser-stored credentials, UI logout/family revocation, forged-localStorage denial, coordinated tabs, outage/recovery and wrong-state rejection. Credential verification uses a bounded disposable HTTP fixture, not a deployed authentication-service; unrelated dashboard APIs are explicitly fixture-mocked. HTTP loopback tests do not verify external TLS termination.
- Five existing fixture-backed browser layout/drivers tests now mock the session broker explicitly rather than seed fake authenticated localStorage. They are not additional real OAuth login proofs. Browser discovery supports an explicit executable, installed Playwright Chromium or existing macOS Chrome without a local global installation. CI installs its own ephemeral Chromium. Native tests own/clean their clusters, schemas, keys, browser/server processes and separate Next build/TypeScript-config artifacts.
- Latest targeted gates: 228 issuer, 90 gateway, 361 policy, 92 identity, 32 adapter, 413 frontend Jest, 34 browser mediation, 80 license PostgreSQL, 25 identity PostgreSQL, 23 cross-service token/session/key PostgreSQL, four browser/PostgreSQL and five fixture browser tests. These are focused regression gates, not a successful whole-repository production build or deployment. The same 18 unrelated frontend TypeScript diagnostics remain.
- Next priorities remain authoritative account disable/password/role/PIM generation fencing, owner-scoped browser session administration, consistent receiving-service/tenant/resource authorization and eventually signed lifecycle/outbox/vendor provisioning. Browser sign-in is not automatic identity linking, least-privilege role issuance for every service, Microsoft 365 license assignment or a completed Entra replacement.

## Fifteenth Implemented Increment: Live Directory Authorization Fences

### Canonical State, Not a Second Session Store

Human OAuth authorization now requires an explicit `(issuer, subject) → canonical user UUID` link and an enabled user in identity-service. Credential verification alone is not enough. Unknown, unlinked, disabled or deleted identities receive no usable authorization code/family. Subjects are not guessed from usernames, email, UUID-looking claims or caller-supplied role/profile fields. Application sessions remain a separate namespace; this increment does not fabricate directory users or application permissions for them.

Identity migration `005_authorization_generation.sql` adds durable, database-generated user and link revisions. User creation, enabled-state changes and explicit security resets advance the user revision. Every new link advances a separate link revision, including unlink/relink to the same user. Profile-only edits preserve the revision. Callers cannot choose/reset an old generation by supplying the column value; direct revision changes allocate a new sequence value. Existing user UUID tombstones still prevent delete/recreate reuse. Reapplying the migration preserves existing revisions.

The issuer captures this minimal authorization context after verified credential/MFA sign-in, stores it with authorization/device grants and signs it into human tokens. Refresh snapshots already persist immutably in PostgreSQL. Code exchange, approved-device redemption and refresh must still match the original canonical user/revisions; they cannot silently upgrade an old authentication checkpoint to a current generation. Async refresh checks run before credential consumption, and snapshot-generation replacement is rejected by SessionStore. Parallel device approvals recheck the current record after the new asynchronous checkpoint instead of overwriting an already approved user.

The live issuer access-state check now includes this fresh identity source read, with no positive state cache or memory/network-failure fallback. Its existing consumers—core gateway, identity-service and policy-service—therefore deny the old human token on their next authorization check, including direct API access and license allocation. Idle native WebSockets retain the existing five-second state poll; browser pages recheck on visibility/30-second heartbeat, bootstrap or refresh. Reactivation, relinking or a new login do not make the previous context valid again.

This is authorization-generation fencing, not a distributed delete of every OAuth session row. An invalidated family's persisted `revoked_at` may remain null; its old signed/snapshotted context is nonetheless unusable. Administrative family deletion/retention and an effective-status session viewer remain separate work. Already authorized/in-flight operations are not retroactively cancelled, and the source read is not a cross-database transaction with a later resource mutation. Availability/load and target-system enforcement still require production verification.

### Scoped Workload Read Without Recursion

The issuer reads `POST /internal/identity/authorization-state` at a fixed `OAUTH_IDENTITY_AUTHORITY_URL`. Sending a normal user bearer there would recurse: identity's normal guard calls issuer access-state, which now needs identity. Instead the issuer signs a short-lived, read-only workload assertion using its existing protected signing key; no private material or shared administrative secret is copied to identity-service.

The assertion has a dedicated `opendirectory-identity-authority` audience, `od-identity-authority+jwt` type, `identity-authorization-request` purpose, `oauth-provider` subject, workload actor and exact `identity.authorization.read` scope. Method/path and the selected subject are signed; the request accepts only that one subject, with issuer inferred from the verified assertion. Identity independently verifies RS256/issuer/JWKS, exact purpose/actor/audience/type, bounded lifetime/iat/JTI, target binding and allowed headers before the isolated read. User/admin/API tokens, query selectors, extra body fields and alternate methods do not acquire this privilege. The normal API middleware does not gain an internal-token bypass. Distinct validation rules address cross-token substitution as discussed in [RFC 8725, section 3.12](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8725.html#section-3.12); this private request protocol is not a general OAuth workload grant or a claim of full standard conformance.

Responses contain only issuer, subject, active state and the three-field canonical context, never credentials, profile attributes or roles. Database failure is 503. The issuer bounds fetch/response size, denies redirects and aborts after 1.5 seconds; identity performs a read-only transaction with a one-second server-side statement timeout so stalled queries are cancelled rather than occupying a pool connection indefinitely. Malformed/mismatched responses and workload authentication/configuration errors are 503, not an eligible identity. Refresh consumption rolls back on outage so a recovered source can redeem the same still-unspent credential. Assertions last at most 30 seconds; their read-only target-bound replay is not prevented by a nonce database. Verification keys have a bounded 30-second cache, not a live workload revocation service. Protect the fixed source transport with HTTPS/mTLS or an appropriately isolated trusted network; local HTTP tests do not prove production transport security.

### Narrow Invalidation and Device Separation

`POST /api/users/:canonicalUUID/authorization/invalidate` performs a manual security reset with no body/query selectors. Only `users.sessions.invalidate`—the narrow `session-administrator` role or existing global administration—permits it. It does not grant user/profile writes, account enable/disable, link changes or directory-wide reads. The Next.js rewrite explicitly reaches canonical identity rather than the older user backend. The database transaction records verified actor, reason, revision and enabled state in append-only authorization audit; audit failure rolls back the revision change. Existing canonical account-status writes also advance the generation atomically and retain their separate status-management permission.

Issuer userinfo/introspection/management now require the API audience and explicit access purpose, not merely any signed non-ID JWT. This is necessary to keep the new workload assertions out of ordinary APIs. Newly enrolled device credentials consequently have an explicit device audience/purpose and a separate self-device verifier on the four agent routes. They cannot read another device's queue or administer the issuer; ordinary nonadmin user tokens also cannot use those self-agent routes. Long-lived device credential rotation/revocation/state remains a separate lifecycle, not canonical human-user governance. Legacy audience-less device credentials must be replaced by reviewed re-enrollment; no broad JWT fallback is restored.

### Breaking Upgrade and Bootstrap

- Apply identity migration 005 and provision/review canonical user rows and explicit issuer/subject links **before** enabling this issuer version. Existing access tokens and refresh/code/device checkpoints without a valid context are denied, not backfilled from a current directory lookup. Reauthenticate human principals and reissue affected narrow worker credentials; do not relax the new checks to make old credentials work.
- For an existing deployment, prepare mappings through the previous authorized identity-link API and normal reviewed migration process. Greenfield/recovery bootstrap requires an independent, protected operator path: pre-provision the verified upstream identity/roles, canonical directory row and reviewed link using controlled database/import tooling. This does not create an administrator from email, seed a universal admin or expose a public bootstrap endpoint. Once operating, use the guarded link/status APIs. A disabled sole administrator cannot re-enable itself with its now-denied token; maintain an independently authorized recovery operator/procedure.
- Configure the issuer's fixed `OAUTH_IDENTITY_AUTHORITY_URL`; both Compose variants default to the internal identity-service endpoint. Identity must trust the same issuer/public JWKS. The assertion is narrowly verified before normal user-token middleware, so booting the issuer before identity or vice versa does not create an authentication recursion; human authentication fails closed until the authority is actually ready. Liveness alone is not a full dependency-readiness proof.
- This source is canonical identity-service state. Authentication-service, SCIM prototypes and legacy browser user-management flows still have independent stores/routes. Changing only those independent flags is not automatically a canonical account disable. Likewise password reset, role changes and PIM transitions are not yet wired to advance this generation atomically or supply authoritative current roles. The manual reset is not a substitute for completing those integrations. User-management UI consolidation is the next required step, followed by credential/role/PIM source unification.
- No outbox dispatcher, off-host immutable archive, automatic identity linking, bulk retention, general service-principal permissions, tenant/resource leases or Microsoft Graph license provisioning is implied. Fresh checks add dependency traffic and signing/SQL cost; the existing identity request-rate budget and pool/timeout settings need load testing and workload-aware operational sizing before production. Do not replace fresh checks with a permissive cache to conceal overload. Database-owner powers and sequence/backup administration still require protected operational controls. No production secrets, accounts, deployments or database state were modified during development.

### Evidence

- Issuer Jest: 254 tests, including assertion binding, no production bypass, strict context parsing, source timeout/outage, no guessed mapping, refresh-generation immutability and distinct device/API token permissions. Identity Jest: 113 tests, including genuinely signed workload requests, audience/type/purpose/method/path/subject/expiry denial, verifier-source outage, minimal projections and narrow invalidation attribution.
- Cross-service PostgreSQL/HTTP gate: 37 tests exercise the actual issuer, gateway, identity and policy services. Added cases cover canonical status changes through the real guarded API, existing/fresh/refresh denial, blocked license writes, reactivation/relink nonresurrection, stale code/device checkpoints, narrow reset/audit rollback, source loss/recovery without spending refresh, server-side stalled-query cancellation, assertion substitution, migration/revision fences, deletion and idle WebSocket shutdown. Concurrent device approval has one winner. Credential verification remains an isolated HTTP fixture.
- Real Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/PostgreSQL gate: five tests now include committed disablement, browser sign-out, restored old-cookie denial after reactivation and fresh verified sign-in. This is not a deployed credential service or an external TLS deployment; unrelated dashboard data is still explicitly fixture-mocked.
- Regression gates also cover 90 gateway, 361 policy, 32 canonical adapter, 414 frontend Jest, 34 mediation-core, 80 license PostgreSQL, 25 identity PostgreSQL and five fixture-backed browser tests. The same 18 unrelated TypeScript diagnostics remain. Compose configuration and syntax checks are not image builds, cluster rollouts or whole-repository certification.
- Two existing HTTP-test harness cases intermittently failed during parallel validation: a gateway permission test returned 401 instead of 403, and an identity CRUD case reported a socket hang-up. Unchanged full-suite reruns passed, including two consecutive gateway runs. Their underlying harness instability was not fixed or presented as production verification by this increment.

## Sixteenth Implemented Increment: Canonical User Console and Conditional Writes

### One Browser Directory, Explicit Capabilities

The main `/users` console now reads and mutates canonical identity-service users. Next.js sends `/api/users` and **all** its subpaths, and `/api/groups` and its subpaths, to identity-service instead of api-backend. Unsupported legacy user/role paths fail at the canonical service rather than falling back to a second user store. Existing OU, license-snapshot and identity-link routes retain their canonical owner.

The console first obtains verified `/api/identity/access` capabilities. Ordinary users do not fetch the administrative user list. A directory reader sees genuine enabled/disabled state, canonical UUIDs and profile attributes but no administrative actions. Creation, profile editing, status changes, security reset and deletion use their separate server-issued permissions; status changes still require both update and status-management grants. A reset-only operator can act on a known canonical UUID without gaining a directory/profile read. Self-reset/disable/delete rechecks the real browser session rather than leaving a fabricated authenticated page.

The old eight sample users, default administrator/role/MFA projections, LLDAP fallback on API failure and success-only buttons are removed from the main user console. Empty results remain empty; unknown capabilities, malformed results, 403 and source failures are not presented as successful user data. Profile/status/delete mutations are confirmed only from a valid canonical response, never optimistic local status flips. Uncertain/stale outcomes disable blind retries and require a reload; mutation requests are not automatically replayed.

Global user search checks canonical read permission and uses the bounded canonical search endpoint, without an alternate LLDAP read. Quick-action navigation uses identity permissions independently of the legacy onboarding grant/service. The Settings user-management entry now opens the same console; the separate quick-onboarding wizard is no longer a main-shell user-creation entry point. The explicitly labeled LLDAP provider integration and its provider-specific setup wizard remain separate and link to the canonical console. Their source/backend orchestration and other integration-specific callers remain unmigrated; this increment does not claim a complete cross-provider onboarding saga or uniform authorization at those other receivers.

### Safe Directory Records, Not Credential Provisioning

New API-created records start **disabled**, with server-generated UUIDs and no credentials, roles, MFA state or automatic issuer/subject link. The editor accepts only username, email, display name, department and title. Unsupported IDs, activation flags, role/password fields, non-scalar data and control characters are rejected server-side. Profile updates send only their supplied fields; they do not rewrite a stale copy of `enabled` or unmentioned profile data.

Creation-only operators receive the confirmed new record ID without reading unrelated users. A status-authorized operator can separately activate a reviewed record, but that is not provisioning an authentication account. The canonical username is currently a directory attribute, not an automatic rename of the independent credential-store login. Email/username uniqueness across independent providers is not established by this feature; canonical UUIDs and explicitly approved links remain the identity authority. Unsupported password/MFA/role/device management is described honestly instead of simulated.

The same canonical UUID is usable by the existing license principal/eligibility workflow. Account disablement advances existing authorization/eligibility fences; reactivation does not automatically restore old sessions or released assignments. External credentials, Microsoft 365/vendor entitlements, group nesting and lifecycle/outbox dispatch are not silently modified by console success.

### Record Versions and Transactional Audit

Migration `006_directory_user_records.sql` adds server-generated `record_revision`, stable pagination ordering/indexing and an append-only user-record audit. Record changes—including security-generation updates—advance the version; migration reapplication preserves existing data and versions. Audits contain verified actor, action, version and an explicit before/after profile/status projection, not arbitrary request fields or credentials. Source changes and their audit commit in the same transaction. Audit failure cannot report a saved profile, disable or deletion. This remains protected database evidence with PII/retention obligations, not an off-host immutable archive.

`GET /api/users/:UUID`, creation and update return an exact record-version ETag. User rows in list responses also include their revision. **PUT and DELETE require one exact strong `If-Match` user ETag**: missing preconditions return 428; weak/wildcard/multiple or malformed validators return 400; a changed version returns 412. These are a deliberately bounded mutation contract, not an implementation of every generic HTTP conditional/cache combination. Conditional updates prevent the lost-update problem described by [RFC 9110](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9110.html#section-13.1.1); mandatory preconditions use [RFC 6585's 428 response](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6585.html#section-3).

The service locks the row and checks its current version before applying a field-specific update/delete. Two writers holding one version have one winner; the loser cannot silently overwrite the newer record. This also fixes a security-relevant preexisting race: a profile-only writer previously merged a prior read of the whole row and could restore an old `enabled=true` after another operator disabled the account, even without a status grant. Status is now both independently authorized and excluded from profile-only SQL; a stale form is rejected instead of re-enabling the user.

List/search/filter/pagination are validated and performed against PostgreSQL with a consistent read snapshot. Defaults are page 1/25 rows, with at most 100 rows and a bounded page/search range. Search includes username and profile fields with literal substring semantics; a failed query never downgrades into a successful demo mirror. The explicitly nonproduction in-memory mode remains clearly labeled and is not durable governance. Large-directory search/indexing, workload-aware rate budgets and operational performance still need load verification.

### Migration, Evidence and Open Work

- Apply identity migration 006 before serving the updated browser/service. Existing canonical IDs, enabled flags and approved subject links are not guessed or remapped. Old PUT/DELETE clients must first read the canonical record and pass its current ETag; there is no unconditional-write compatibility escape. Raw integration callers must also adopt the disabled-by-default create contract and strict fields/query shapes.
- The profile/status console does not unify authentication-service, SCIM prototypes or vendor credential lifecycles. Prepare explicit identity mappings as described in increment fifteen; creation/activation alone does not permit sign-in. The manual reset remains different from password reset and role/PIM propagation. Separate legacy provisioning APIs and existing non-versioned event consumers still need canonical migration, trustworthy ordered outbox delivery, failure compensation and live role-source integration.
- Identity Jest: 130 tests; identity PostgreSQL/HTTP: 32 tests. Added coverage includes strict create/query/precondition contracts, one-winner concurrent updates, prevention of stale re-enable, separate status permissions, audit rollback/immutability, disabled creation and migration reapplication. Existing source/token-generation gates remain in place.
- Frontend Jest: 442 tests. Added coverage exercises capability gating, no sample/fallback data, true disabled state, paginated filters, narrow create/reset permissions, exact-version writes, explicit confirmations, delayed-response selection isolation, unsupported/uncertain responses and no automatic mutation retry. Rewrite tests cover all canonical user/group paths; the legacy user wizard is not used by these entry points.
- The actual Chrome → Next.js → issuer → identity/PostgreSQL gate expands to 13 tests. In addition to the prior login/family tests, real user-console cases verify read-only denial, disabled creation, profile edits and audit, independent operator disable/reactivate with victim-cookie nonresurrection, stale-form 412, reset without listing users, self-reset sign-out, separate deletion authority/tombstones and permission-bound global search. A deliberately distinguishable legacy user backend records zero user requests. The console screenshot is inspected from an owned disposable test context. Credential verification remains an isolated HTTP fixture; unrelated dashboard data is explicitly mocked. This is not a real credential-service/vendor deployment.
- Other targeted gates remain 254 issuer, 90 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 34 mediation-core, 80 license PostgreSQL, 37 cross-service token/session/directory-state tests and five fixture-backed browser layout tests. The same 18 unrelated TypeScript diagnostics remain. Existing intermittent HTTP-test harness issues from increment fifteen are not claimed fixed. Compose/syntax checks do not prove an image build, Helm topology or production rollout.

Next required work is canonical credential/provisioning and password/role/PIM source integration, followed by effective-status session administration, remaining receiving-service/tenant scopes and vendor-backed license fulfillment. The user console is now real and permission-bound; it is not a finished Entra replacement.

## Seventeenth Implemented Increment: Canonical, Expiring Runtime Roles

### One Human OIDC Role Authority

Human OIDC authorization-code and device sign-ins now obtain their roles from persistent canonical identity-service assignments, not from `authentication-service`'s independent role/permission response. Password/MFA verification still belongs to that separate credential service; its successful authentication identifies an issuer subject but cannot manufacture administrator or license-management authority. The issuer replaces both role and permission claims before scope delegation and before storing a pending grant. The explicit, forbidden-in-production issuer demo mode retains its old fixture behavior; normal startup has no credential-role compatibility fallback.

The fixed catalog in `packages/directory-role-contract.js` covers the currently delegated directory, license, application, device-enrollment, policy, operations and gateway roles. `user-profile-editor` provides only canonical profile read/update. Permission claims are derived from the catalog, not caller-supplied permission strings. Existing receiving-service role mappings continue to define their supported operations. This is not custom-role authoring, Microsoft role/API compatibility, a complete cross-service permission registry, tenant/resource scoping, or an authorization migration for independent legacy JWT/SAML/provisioning paths.

The signed internal workload read remains read-only and subject-bound. Eligible responses use schema version 2 and include the canonical active role set plus an exact fingerprint of active immutable assignment IDs/roles. `identity_authorization.rolesRevision` is required even when a client does not request role claims. Old three-field contexts are rejected, not grandfathered. A disabled, missing or unlinked user is still ineligible independently of roles. A user with no grants can sign in as an ordinary user; no administrator or synthetic default role is created.

### Bounded, Independently Delegated Administration

Migration `007_directory_roles.sql` adds immutable role assignments and append-only role audit. `role-reader` has `identities.roles.read`; `role-administrator` adds `identities.roles.manage` but no user-profile read/write, credential, status, link or license-allocation permission of its own. Existing user/session/license operators cannot assign roles merely because they administer their own area. API writes require a verified, bound human operator; workload tokens and unbound legacy administrators are rejected. Canonical UUID comparison prevents self-administration through another linked subject or different UUID casing.

- `GET /api/identity/roles/catalog` returns supported roles, protected flags and caller-specific assignment capability.
- `GET /api/users/:UUID/roles` reads at most 100 assignments, active first, with explicit truncation and source-computed active/expired/revoked status. It does not read a user profile or expose credentials.
- `POST /api/users/:UUID/roles` accepts exactly `role`, `durationSeconds` and `reason`. Lifetimes are 60–28,800 seconds, with a 10–500-character explanation. Permanent/future-start/extra-permission/actor fields are not supported. A per-user lock serializes the 100-active-assignment bound.
- `DELETE /api/users/:UUID/roles/:assignmentUUID` performs idempotent one-way revocation. Assignment identity, role, reason and expiry cannot be edited or extended; replacing an expired grant requires a new, separately audited assignment. Both assignment and canonical target must match.
- Only a canonical global administrator may grant/revoke protected `admin` and `role-administrator` roles on **another** user. Delegated role administrators cannot delegate their own administrative authority or turn it into global administration. This still permits sensitive non-global role delegation and must be operated as a privileged function.

Role creation/revocation, target authorization-generation advancement and actor-attributed audit commit atomically. Failed role or user audit rolls back the entire operation. Role records and audit survive user deletion; admission locks require an existing canonical user, and existing deleted-ID tombstones forbid identity resurrection. The retained records have retention/PII obligations and are not an off-host immutable archive. Expiry is computed from the trusted database clock; it is not a background worker's best-effort timer and does not itself emit a separate expiry audit event.

### Live Expiry and Nonresurrection

The issuer's fresh access-state check compares both the canonical generation/fingerprint and actual scope-appropriate role/permission claims. Creating or revoking a grant fences existing user sessions and pending code/device grants. Expiry removes its assignment from the live fingerprint without needing a write or worker. Core gateway, direct identity-service and direct policy-service reject the old access token; refresh cannot spend a credential to resurrect the expired authorization. Idle native gateway WebSockets also close at the existing authority-poll interval. A later matching role grant has a new ID and generation and cannot revive the original access/refresh pair.

This deliberately requires fresh sign-in after role changes, rather than silently extending an old refresh family's authority. It also fences scopes without a role claim. These guarantees apply at the already integrated receivers, not every historical service, open federated session or operation already authorized before a concurrent change. Shared trusted clock operation, load/rate limits, remaining receivers and per-tenant audiences still require operational work.

### Real Console and Explicit Bootstrap

`/roles` now uses verified identity capabilities, the canonical catalog and a known canonical user UUID. The old sample global administrators/custom-role counts, legacy `/api/auth/roles` fallback and fabricated save/delete actions are removed. The view does not fetch the directory user list, so a role operator gains no unrelated profile read. Protected choices follow source-issued capabilities; self-writes are not offered. Grant/revoke require an explicit confirmation and a valid canonical receipt, without optimistic success or automatic mutation retry. Source failures/malformed responses do not become sample data. Uncertain writes require reloading and reviewing the actual assignments before another attempt; the API does not promise retry-idempotent creation.

No existing credential role is imported automatically. Before enabling this issuer/service version, review canonical subject links and explicitly bootstrap the intended recovery/administration accounts using privileged, separate identity-database access:

Coordinate the issuer and identity-service upgrade across replicas: the eligible workload-response schema changes from 1 to 2, and old three-field token contexts stop authorizing. Mixed old/new authority versions fail closed rather than inheriting old privileges. Apply migration 007, provision reviewed grants, then verify fresh sign-in and live reads before routing production traffic. This is a breaking authorization migration, not a rolling compatibility fallback.

```sh
IDENTITY_BOOTSTRAP_DATABASE_URL="$REVIEWED_IDENTITY_DATABASE_URL" \
  node services/core/identity-service/scripts/bootstrap-role.js \
  CANONICAL_USER_UUID admin --permanent "Reviewed emergency administration bootstrap"
```

The command requires an explicit database URL, existing canonical UUID, catalog role, lifetime and reason. It applies migrations, never creates a user/credential/link, records an `offline-bootstrap` audit actor and advances the authorization generation. `--permanent` is restricted to offline `admin`/`role-administrator` recovery; ordinary API grants remain bounded. For a bounded bootstrap replace `--permanent` with seconds (60–28,800). Store the database credential through protected operational provisioning, not a checked-in environment file or copied shell history. Maintain independently reviewed recovery access; this helper is not an online bootstrap bypass, automatic break-glass policy or credential/MFA enrollment. Existing accounts must reauthenticate after migration/bootstrap.

### Evidence and Remaining Work

The real PostgreSQL/HTTP suites cover permission separation, canonical self-target denial, protected delegation, strict lifetime/field contracts, one-way immutable rows, concurrent bounds, transaction rollback, deletion retention, reviewed bootstrap and migration reapplication. The actual issuer/gateway/identity/policy gate poisons the credential service's role/permission response, verifies actual canonical license allocation, checks denied use after role revoke/expiry, pending-grant fences, refresh nonconsumption during source outage and idle WebSocket closure. Short-lived SQL fixture grants make real expiry observable without weakening the production API's 60-second minimum.

The actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/PostgreSQL gate also poisons credential roles, exercises the canonical role console and verifies operator/victim sign-out, fresh canonical claims, revoke audit, self/ordinary-user denial and zero legacy user-backend reads. Credential verification remains a disposable HTTP fixture, not a real credential-service deployment. Frontend tests exercise confirmations, unconfirmed outcomes, no blind retry and no profile-read escalation. Screenshots are inspected from owned disposable browser contexts.

Current targeted gates: 260 issuer, 157 identity, 90 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 454 frontend unit/API tests; 39 identity, 80 license and 42 actual issuer/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 15 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity tests and five fixture-backed layout tests (1,569 total). The same 18 unrelated frontend TypeScript diagnostics remain; no clean full frontend build is claimed. Prior HTTP-harness flakiness is not claimed fixed. Configuration/syntax checks are not a container build, live credential-service test or deployment.

This implements direct, time-bounded **active** role grants, not PIM eligibility/request/independent approval, activation-time MFA/conditional access, custom permission authoring, group/dynamic roles, access reviews, tenant/resource delegation, or automatic vendor/license fulfillment. The legacy PIM and credential services must be integrated into this authority rather than becoming competing runtime grants. Effective-status session administration, signed lifecycle delivery and complete receiving-service enforcement remain open. No production data, key material, live accounts or deployment is changed by the tests.

The design follows the distinct permission/scope/time dimensions described in [Microsoft's least-privilege role guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/role-based-access-control/best-practices); scope- and approval-based governance are explicitly not yet implemented here.

## Eighteenth Implemented Increment: Eligible Roles, Independent Review and Real Activation

### Canonical PIM, Not Another Role Store

The main `/pim` console now implements an actual eligible → requested → independently approved → owner-activated lifecycle in identity-service. Approval alone does **not** grant a role. Successful activation inserts a bounded row into the same immutable `identity_role_assignments` authority used by human OIDC sign-in and live access-state checks. It does not set an unrelated prototype request to “active,” guess an LDAP group, or invent a second bearer-token role source. The actual license API is exercised before approval, after approval, after activation/new sign-in and after revocation.

This is an additional eligible-role path, not a claim that all active grants require PIM: separately authorized direct role assignment and reviewed offline recovery from increment seventeen remain available. Role-level mandatory-PIM policies, step-up assurance and tenant/resource scoping are still required before claiming comprehensive privileged-access governance.

### Separate Responsibilities and Owner-Scoped Self-Service

- `pim-administrator` has eligibility read/manage only: it cannot review requests, read the general user directory, directly assign active roles, or allocate licenses merely by holding that role.
- `pim-approver` has request read/review and activation revocation, not eligibility/profile/active-role management. `pim-reader` has eligibility/request reads only. Both the PIM administrative and approver roles are protected catalog roles; only a canonical global administrator can delegate them through the direct-role API. Offline permanent bootstrap remains restricted to the existing emergency `admin`/`role-administrator` options.
- Eligibility cannot be created or administratively withdrawn for the operator's own canonical UUID. Protected roles additionally require a global administrator for eligibility creation, review and administrative revocation. Catalog write/review flags are calculated by the receiver, not inferred from browser role labels.
- Any verified, enabled, bound human can read their own eligibility/requests and request activation **only** for their own existing eligibility. The beneficiary is derived from the authenticated canonical context. Workloads, unbound legacy identities, supplied beneficiary/reviewer/approval fields and query-selector aliases do not become PIM authority.
- The canonical beneficiary cannot approve, deny or administratively revoke their own request, regardless of another subject spelling or held administrative role. The owner can separately cancel their own request/deactivate their own lease. Review does not imply permission to activate another user's request.

All PIM data is persistent PostgreSQL state; there is no in-memory mirror/failure fallback, including in directory demo mode. Reads are bounded to 100 items with truncation information; eligible/actionable self items precede history, and the review queue prioritizes pending items. Manager reads use a known canonical UUID without granting profile-list access. These are service-wide read/delegation permissions, **not** tenant/object-level isolation or an unbounded directory-scale report.

### State, Time and Atomic Evidence

Migration `008_privileged_activation.sql` adds immutable eligibility/request evidence, append-only PIM audit and a unique immutable request origin on canonical role assignments. APIs live below `/api/identity/pim/`, already routed to identity-service by Next.js:

| Operation | Contract |
| --- | --- |
| `GET catalog`, `GET self` | Bound-human catalog capabilities and owner-only records; no general directory read. |
| `GET eligibilities?userId=:UUID` | Separately authorized eligibility read for a known canonical user. |
| `POST eligibilities` | Exactly `userId`, catalog `role`, `maxDurationSeconds`, `validForSeconds`, `reason`; recipient must exist and be enabled. |
| `DELETE eligibilities/:UUID` | One-way withdrawal, ending pending approvals and linked active canonical leases in the same transaction. |
| `GET requests` | Separately authorized bounded review/history queue. |
| `POST requests` | Exactly a client-generated request `id`, own `eligibilityId`, `durationSeconds`, `reason`; beneficiary/approval cannot be supplied. |
| `POST requests/:UUID/approve` or `/deny` | Empty body; independent live reviewer, one pending decision winner. |
| `POST requests/:UUID/activate` | Empty body; approved owner only, fresh authentication and live reviewer authority rechecked before the canonical grant. |
| `POST requests/:UUID/cancel` or `/revoke` | Empty body; owner cancellation/deactivation or separately authorized independent administrative withdrawal. |

Eligibility is finite (60 seconds to 30 days); activation duration is 60–28,800 seconds and cannot exceed the eligibility's maximum. Requests require a 10–500-character explanation. Pending requests expire after at most 24 hours, no later than eligibility expiry. An approval has a five-minute activation window. Activation requires an issuer-verified `auth_time` within five minutes; rotating an old refresh credential does not constitute a new authentication. The lease is capped by the remaining eligibility lifetime, with at least 60 seconds remaining required before activation. Expired eligibility/approval is never silently extended. Current UI uses days/minutes; the API's exact policy values are seconds.

Request UUIDs bind immutable owner, eligibility, reason and requested duration. A repeat of the same request UUID returns its actual state without another request/audit entry; changing its inputs conflicts. Concurrent distinct requests cannot open a second current activation for the same eligibility. Concurrent reviewers have one decision winner. A repeat activation can report the same existing lease after new authentication, never extend it or create another. Terminal decisions and revoked eligibility/leases cannot be revived; a new request/eligibility requires a new identity and fresh review.

Mutation transactions lock affected canonical users in deterministic order, then eligibility/request records. They recheck the caller's current enabled user, original issuer/subject link, authorization generation and effective-role fingerprint from canonical SQL state, not merely an old JWT role snapshot. Requester generation/link/role changes make the pending checkpoint stale. Before activation, the original reviewer's canonical user/link/generation/effective roles and review permission must still be valid; reviewer role expiry is detected even without a user-generation update. Ordinary reviewer sign-out is not a retroactive erasure of an already recorded decision; the decision is tied to canonical authorization state, not a retained bearer credential.

Activation inserts the canonical role row and transitions the request atomically with role, user-generation and PIM audit. Eligibility withdrawal atomically cancels pending/approved requests and revokes all linked leases; direct revocation of a linked canonical role also updates its PIM request. Failed audit cannot acknowledge an approval, activate a role, withdraw eligibility or partially revoke leases. SQL guards reject self-review, invalid state transitions, snapshot/expiry/origin rewrites, unapproved linked grants and destructive evidence edits. PIM audit contains verified canonical actor and immutable request/decision context, never raw access/refresh credentials. It contains personal/governance data and still needs retention controls and off-host tamper-resistant archival.

The role write advances the existing authorization fence. Old access/refresh and pending issuer grants cannot inherit the new privilege; the owner must sign in again. Deactivation/withdrawal also fences old tokens at the issuer, core gateway and direct identity/policy receivers. Normal canonical role expiry handles lease expiry without a worker; a later grant cannot resurrect an old pair. Other receivers, operations already authorized before a concurrent change and independent legacy credentials/federated sessions are not covered by this claim.

Assignment state is not a promise that sign-in is currently permitted. Disabling/resetting an account fences access and pending checkpoints, but is not automatic deletion of every already committed role assignment. An unexpired activated lease remains assigned until explicit withdrawal/expiry; a subsequently enabled and newly authenticated user may still receive that assignment. End the activation or eligibility when the privilege itself must be removed. This distinction also applies to separately managed direct grants.

### Console, Migration and Evidence

The main PIM view uses canonical capabilities and real source responses for eligibility, requests, independent review, activation and revocation. It never treats an approval as active access. It does not call the legacy `/api/pim` group/request store or fetch the user directory to administer a known UUID. Self-review/self-eligibility controls are absent, every mutation requires confirmation, and malformed/uncertain outcomes cannot show success or trigger blind retries. A stale authentication response offers interactive sign-in rather than falsely treating refresh as step-up. Confirmed self activation/deactivation rechecks the actual browser session.

The old main-console prototype tabs for arbitrary PIM groups, independent session recording and break-glass operations are no longer presented as canonical privileged-role controls. Their backend services and noncanonical API routes still exist and require explicit authorization/data migration or retirement; their resource side effects are not automatically imported, revoked or represented by this new workflow. This removes a misleading primary entry point, not those independent runtime surfaces.

Apply identity migration 008 before exposing the updated PIM API/console. Existing direct grants remain intact with a null PIM origin; the workload/token-context format remains schema 2. No legacy role/group IDs, eligibility, queued requests or approval actors are automatically mapped. Review canonical identity links and delegate the newly protected PIM staff roles through a verified global administrator, with bounded duration. No production database, credential, live account or vendor is changed by the tests.

Identity PostgreSQL/HTTP coverage includes responsibility separation, ownership and injected-field denial, UUID retry binding, independent decisions, fresh-owner activation, concurrent decision/activation, audit rollback, active-lease withdrawal, immutable snapshots/origins, migration reapplication and requester/reviewer role-expiry fences. Short-lived, explicitly seeded SQL fixture roles prove live expiry without weakening production duration contracts. The actual issuer → gateway/direct identity/policy gate verifies real license allocation only after approved activation/new sign-in, denial after revoke/eligibility withdrawal and rollback when PIM audit is unavailable. Actual Chrome → Next → issuer/identity/PostgreSQL tests exercise the full three-actor workflow, source roles, forced reauthentication, self-review/self-eligibility denial and zero legacy PIM/profile-list requests. Credential verification remains an isolated HTTP fixture, not deployed credential/MFA/vendor infrastructure. Screenshots are inspected from owned disposable test contexts.

Current targeted gates: 260 issuer, 187 identity, 90 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 467 frontend unit/API tests; 50 identity, 80 license and 45 actual issuer/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 17 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity tests and five fixture-backed layout tests (1,628 total). Removing the old main PIM view also removes its preexisting `unknown`-as-ReactNode diagnostic; the remaining 17 unrelated TypeScript diagnostics match the prior baseline. A clean full frontend build, container image/Helm rollout, complete legacy-backend retirement and production credential/MFA/vendor integration are not claimed. Prior HTTP harness flakiness is not claimed fixed.
