Still missing: verified activation-time MFA/`acr`/`amr` assurance and conditional-access policy, mandatory-PIM role policy, configurable/multistage approval groups, eligible-role reviews/renewal workflows, tenant/resource limits, alerts/delivery, risk-based activation, scoped emergency access, canonical credential provisioning/password propagation, complete receiver enforcement and vendor-backed license fulfillment. Fresh password authentication is **not** represented as proven MFA. These remain part of the original platform goal, not optional completeness claims.

The workflow borrows the eligible/active distinction and independent-review concepts documented by [Microsoft's role activation guidance](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/id-governance/privileged-identity-management/pim-resource-roles-activate-your-roles) and [approval workflow](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/id-governance/privileged-identity-management/pim-resource-roles-approval-workflow); it is not an implementation of Azure resource scopes, Microsoft APIs or their full policy behavior.

## Nineteenth Implemented Increment: Live Actor-Preserving Orchestration

### Authorization at Every Step

Quick-actions is now a fourth live-checking resource boundary, including direct access to port 3950. Its private routes verify RS256, the configured issuer and shared API audience, explicit access purpose, expiry, issuing client, session and token IDs, actor type and the canonical human authorization context. ID tokens, retired `svc-` strings, API keys, query credentials, duplicate authorization headers and incomplete legacy claims cannot authorize an operation. Only exact GET/HEAD `/health` is public. Verification/source outages fail closed; a failed live authority is not replaced by a previously successful authorization cache.

The existing default-deny operation registry remains responsible for application management, consent, lifecycle, devices, policies and other task-specific permissions. An application actor cannot inherit human administrator authority from role labels; workloads need explicit operation permissions. Human application readers are not application writers, and a PIM application-management lease is subject to the same canonical expiry/revocation fences as license roles. These remain service-wide permissions, not tenant/object-scoped grants.

Every accepted request receives an immutable, isolated operator context through `AsyncLocalStorage.run`. Each private `serviceClient.call` rechecks fixed issuer access-state before dispatch and forwards that exact original access token, plus one server-generated UUID operation reference. Parallel actors cannot borrow each other's context, and callers cannot override its authorization header. Private calls without a verified request context fail instead of acquiring client credentials. The old `getServiceToken` helper explicitly rejects use; `SERVICE_TOKEN`, `QA_CLIENT_SECRET` and the former service-token bypass are not authorization alternatives. Scheduled/background jobs need their own explicitly authorized identity/workflow; retaining an ended HTTP context is not a background grant.

Downstream service roots are fixed configuration, restricted to plain HTTP(S) origins without user information, query, fragment or path prefix. Requests cannot supply a different origin or follow a redirect with the operator credential. Private methods/paths are constrained; response bodies are bounded to 1 MiB and the complete fetch/read has a ten-second deadline. Public health probes carry no credential and report failures honestly. This is bounded delegation, not authenticated transport, per-resource OAuth audience separation or a complete SSRF/network policy.

### Stop, Do Not Escalate or Pretend to Roll Back

A failed live check, or a receiver's 401/403 at response headers, latches termination of that request context and aborts in-flight fetches. The next step cannot recover into a service identity or continue merely because an unavailable authority later recovers. Closing/aborting the incoming request ends delegation as well. No automatic retry, credential substitution or privileged compensating mutation is added. Successful principal creation, secret rotation and onboarding require another fresh check before their credential fields can be disclosed; incomplete receipts have those fields redacted.

These checks do not atomically commit across services or recall a mutation already accepted by a receiver. A concurrent revocation/disconnect may happen after dispatch, and aborting transport does not prove remote rollback. Already accepted effects remain unknown/partial and require reconciliation. Independent steps already dispatched in parallel cannot be represented as never having happened. The generated operation reference helps correlate a receipt; it is not a persistent idempotency record, durable saga, distributed transaction or append-only operation audit. Existing optional Redis metadata is not promoted to an authorization/operation authority.

The frontend quick-actions client now rejects HTTP 207, explicit unsuccessful receipts and reported bulk failures instead of treating every 2xx as completion. The application wizard requires a confirmed success with actual nonempty client ID, SPN and client secret; it no longer invents a successful identifier/empty secret. A partial/unknown outcome stays on the form with a visible warning and disables resubmission until the operator closes and reviews it. The client does not automatically replay a mutation or infer global logout from every receiving-service denial. This does not yet provide a durable operation-status/reconciliation console, idempotent resume or canonical application/service-principal lifecycle.

### Explicit Packaging and Breaking Migration

The core gateway knows `/api/quick` as a delegated receiver, preserves its complete path and forwards parsed POST bodies. Gateway acceptance does not replace the quick-actions permission checks. An optional `quick-actions` module is disabled by default in `config/modules.yaml`; the two Compose variants package it behind the nondefault `delegated-orchestration` profile. A root-context Node 22 image copies only the service and its shared access-state/context contracts, runs as a nonroot user and needs no issuer private-key mount. Compose additionally uses a read-only filesystem, drops capabilities, forbids privilege escalation and binds its optional host port to loopback. Configuration validation is not a built image or deployment; the local Docker daemon remains unavailable.

Before enabling this profile/module:

1. Deploy the canonical issuer/identity schema and protected keys/links/roles from earlier increments, then reauthenticate; old purpose-less, audience-less or service-string credentials are not migrated.
2. Configure `OIDC_ISSUER`, `OIDC_AUDIENCE`, public `JWKS_URI` and fixed `OIDC_ACCESS_STATE_URL` consistently. Only public verification material belongs in quick-actions. Review routing/network exposure and use protected transport across untrusted hops.
3. Review all nine configured receiver origins: `AUTH_SERVICE_URL`, `DIRECTORY_SERVICE_URL`, `KERBEROS_SERVICE_URL`, `SAMBA_SERVICE_URL`, `DEVICE_SERVICE_URL`, `POLICY_SERVICE_URL`, `PIM_SERVICE_URL`, `MDM_SERVICE_URL` and `APP_STORE_SERVICE_URL`. Compose exposes corresponding `QUICK_ACTIONS_*_URL` overrides; no default legacy endpoint is evidence of compatible receiver authorization.
4. Upgrade each receiver to verify the delegated issuer/purpose/audience, live state and exact operation permission, including its direct API. A service that expects a shared token may now reject delegation; leave the path unavailable rather than restoring a privileged service-token fallback. Enable the module separately only after those receiving boundaries and partial-operation recovery have been reviewed.

This increment does not consolidate legacy AD/Kerberos/credential/PIM stores or register the wizard's application in the canonical OAuth client store. It does not implement Graph/vendor provisioning, receiver-wide permission enforcement, application credential custody/rotation policy, verified activation-time MFA or tenant/resource isolation. Protecting the orchestrator cannot prevent a bypass through a separately exposed, insufficiently guarded legacy receiver.

### Actual Chain Evidence

Quick-actions unit/signed-HTTP tests cover strict claim validation, fixed public JWKS, source failure/recovery, canonical context, no positive authorization cache, app/human separation, context isolation, closed contexts, per-step state changes, denied-header termination before a stalled body, time/size bounds, redirect denial and credential-free health. Real PostgreSQL tests run the actual issuer, identity, gateway, policy and a separate quick-actions process. Recording HTTP receivers validate the original operator through issuer access-state and acknowledge each fixture step; their behavior is not a deployed authentication-service, AD DC or Kerberos KDC.

The real chain proves task-specific reader/writer denial, verified `createdBy`, no service-credential exchange, PIM-governed application delegation, direct-port expiry denial, token/family revocation, mid-workflow source failure and no resumption after recovery, independent parallel actors, disconnect termination and a final credential-disclosure fence. Actual Chrome → Next → issuer/identity/quick-actions tests verify both a complete application receipt and a partial first-step outcome after a canonical security reset. The latter makes exactly one create attempt, exposes no secret/success view and prevents blind resubmission. The partial-warning screenshot is inspected from an owned disposable context; no user profile, live account or production database is modified.

Current targeted gates pass 2,039 tests: 393 quick-actions, 260 issuer, 187 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter and 470 frontend unit/API tests; 50 identity, 80 license and 54 actual issuer/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 19 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. Both optional-profile Compose configurations and YAML parse successfully, and `git diff --check` passes. The frontend-wide type check still fails with the same 17 unrelated baseline diagnostics and no added/removed diagnostic; a clean full build or full-repository test pass is not claimed. Owned processes and temporary SQL clusters are cleaned up; no container build/deployment, real receiver rollout or vendor integration is claimed.

## Twentieth Implemented Increment: Canonical Live OAuth Applications

### One Issuing Application Authority

OAuth/OIDC client registration now lives in `oauth_clients` with a persistent monotonic generation, enabled state, immutable public/confidential authentication type and irreversible deletion evidence. Protocol endpoints read that authority rather than a local client map; separate issuer processes observe the same metadata, enablement, secret and scope changes. Source/migration/validation failures deny access with no formerly successful client snapshot fallback. Explicit development-only `OAUTH_REVOCATION_DEMO_MODE` also marks client responses as demo; it is forbidden in production and is not used by the PostgreSQL/browser gates.

New registrations default to disabled, safe identity scopes and no client-supplied identifier/actor/secret. Workload-only clients can have no redirect; authorization-code clients require registered redirects. Metadata rejects unknown actor/privilege fields, duplicates, unsupported grant types, oversized lists/strings, fragments and URL user information. Callbacks allow HTTPS or explicit loopback HTTP, never arbitrary remote plaintext HTTP. Redirect use still requires exact registered-string matching; this is not full native-app/custom-scheme or pushed-authorization support.

### Delegated Administration Without Scope Takeover

- `application-reader` reads actual safe metadata and capabilities only. It cannot create, enable, rotate, delete or read back a secret and needs no canonical profile-list permission.
- `application-administrator` receives separate `applications.read/create/update/delete/credentials.rotate` operations. A create-only explicit grant cannot use `enabled: true` to bypass update/activation permission.
- Safe delegable scopes here are `openid`, `profile`, `email`, `groups`, `offline_access`, `sessions.self.read` and `sessions.self.revoke`. In particular, `roles` is **not** an ordinary application-administrator grant: it exposes the user's runtime platform roles. Other administrative/custom scopes are protected as well.
- Only a verified canonical human `admin` can create privileged-scope registrations or modify, rotate or delete an existing privileged-scope client. Checking both the current and proposed scopes prevents taking over a protected application by removing its scopes first or rotating its secret/redirect instead of changing grants. `applications.permissions.assign` is reported only to that global actor at this boundary.
- An application actor, an `oauth.admin` scope alone, a realm label or caller-supplied role/actor fields cannot become a human registration administrator. Workload OAuth scope issuance remains constrained by configured grants; this is not a new general workload permission/role issuer.
- The currently issuing client cannot be modified/rotated/deleted using its own bearer context, even by a global administrator. Use an independently provisioned, controlled administrative client to change the browser's own registration; protected-file reconciliation is still required. This avoids invalidating the sole authority needed to acknowledge/disclose its replacement credential.

These are delegated service-wide operations, not tenant/application ownership scopes, app-role consent, user assignment, permission approval or separation of an Entra-style application object from each enterprise service principal. Independent AD/Kerberos/service-account stores used by quick-actions are not imported into this issuer registry. Their separate primary service-principal wizard is not now claimed to register an OAuth client. Other identity-provider prototype tabs remain explicitly labeled as such.

### Persistent Versions, Secret Custody and Evidence

`GET /api/clients/access` exposes verified capabilities; collection reads return `{ clients, truncated }`, bounded to 100, not a pretend complete directory report. Detail reads return safe metadata and an exact `ETag`. Update, secret replacement and deletion require one matching `If-Match`: absent versions return 428, stale/wildcard/multiple conditions 412. The client row is locked while metadata/secret/generation and actor-attributed audit commit together. Every acknowledged change, including a rename, advances the generation; failed audit cannot enable a client, change a credential or acknowledge creation. SQL guards preserve IDs, authentication type, generations, deletion history, the one-time bootstrap marker and append-only application audit. They are not an off-host immutable archive or a substitute for database access control.

Confidential registration and rotation generate 32 random bytes. Only their SHA-256 digest is persisted; the raw generated secret is returned once, only after a final fresh operator-state check. List/detail/audit responses contain neither that secret nor its digest. Public clients have no secret and retain mandatory S256 for authorization-code use. Application management responses are marked `no-store` before the route authentication check. A lost/uncertain create/rotation response cannot recover a readable secret; inspect/reconcile then perform an explicitly authorized replacement rather than automatically replaying the mutation.

This is storage for high-entropy generated application credentials, **not** a password-hashing design for human passwords. Migration hashes existing legacy values without increasing their entropy. Review and rotate weak/reused legacy secrets. Clearing the active plaintext column does not erase historical WAL, snapshots, replicas, logs or backups; handle those separately under the existing secret-retention policy. Hashed storage is not automatic expiry, overlapping credentials, vault/HSM custody, `private_key_jwt`, mTLS or sender-constrained tokens. [PostgreSQL's binary-string documentation](https://www.postgresql.org/docs/16/functions-binarystring.html) documents the built-in digest used by the migration; [OAuth security guidance](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9700.html#section-2.5) recommends asymmetric client authentication, which remains outstanding.

### Live Client Generations, Not Just Future Token Denial

Access/session authorization and refresh snapshots bind a `client_revision` string to the actual issuing client. Login/code/device checkpoints also retain the registration generation. Enablement/configuration changes, rotation and soft deletion therefore fence existing access and refresh as well as pending/approved code/device grants. Restoring identical scopes or re-enabling does not restore an old generation; a new authorized sign-in or workload authentication is required. Refresh source failure does not spend the refresh credential. First-party browser registration is checked against the protected configured secret digest, callbacks and grants on initialization, without silently overwriting a changed/deleted row.

The issuer's live access-state check includes this client generation. Thus the core gateway, direct identity-service, policy-service and quick-actions (including its per-step checks) deny formerly valid application/human tokens after client invalidation. Actual HTTP tests allocate a real local license before disablement and deny subsequent writes through both gateway and direct policy, without another assignment. This does **not** release existing user license entitlements when an OAuth application is disabled; user offboarding and vendor provisioning are separate workflows. A receiver that still validates only an old signature is not brought into compliance by this change. Already authorized/accepted remote effects cannot be recalled or atomically committed with a cross-database source check.

The browser OAuth2/OIDC tab and integrations OAuth tab now share the real registration console. They consume verified capabilities, actual metadata and exact versions, expose one-time receipts only in transient component memory, and confirm activation/disable/rotation/deletion explicitly. Unknown or malformed responses cannot display successful creation, fabricate a secret, overwrite a stale form or trigger blind retry. Secrets are not written to browser storage; closing the receipt or unmounting drops the displayed value, not a promise of immediate JS memory zeroization or XSS immunity. Next.js routes `/api/clients` directly to `OAUTH_PROVIDER_URL` (default `http://oauth-provider:3010`), before the generic backend; issuer-side guards remain authoritative.

### Breaking Migration and Verification Scope

Apply OAuth migration 007 before starting the new issuer. It converts the active legacy `secret` column to `secret_hash`, preserves existing IDs/grants and adds generations/enabled state plus audited soft deletion. Existing clients remain enabled unless previously reviewed otherwise; new registration defaults are disabled. The first nonempty legacy database is marked initialized. A genuinely empty, uninitialized authority seeds only an explicitly configured protected web client and explicitly supplied Grafana/devportal credentials; random/insecure implicit default secrets are retired. Once initialized, even deleting the final client cannot trigger automatic reseeding. An existing registry never gains an unknown configured seed merely because another issuer process starts.

Old tokens and refresh snapshots lack `client_revision` and must reauthenticate; there is no silent generation guess or privileged compatibility bypass. Old client-management callers must adopt the safe collection envelope and conditional writes. Unversioned plaintext client upsert/delete helpers are removed. Pre-migration unsafe remote HTTP callbacks/malformed metadata require reviewed correction rather than automatic weakening. Review bootstrap identities, client scope grants, protected-file deployment and database privileges/backup retention; test an independent recovery client before changing the sign-in registration. Configuration checks are not a production migration or deployment.

Real PostgreSQL tests exercise legacy-secret conversion, concurrent independent registries, conditional-update races, audit rollback, hash-only storage, irreversible evidence, one-time bootstrap, migration reapplication and cancellation/recovery of a blocked SQL read. The actual issuer/gateway/identity/policy/quick-actions gate covers delegated registration/read denial, protected-scope takeover denial, source outages, real local license fencing, old refresh/pending-grant rejection and credential rotation observed by a second fresh issuer process. Chrome/Next tests exercise the canonical registration UI against real issuer/identity/PostgreSQL state; credential verification remains an isolated HTTP fixture, not a deployed password/MFA service. No AD, Kerberos, Graph/vendor credential or live account is provisioned by this work.

The actual browser gate verifies disabled creation, separately confirmed activation, one-time receipt dismissal, secret replacement with rejection of the old credential/token, disablement, audited soft deletion, a human application-reader and stale-form rejection after another real issuer update. Its screenshot is inspected without an exposed secret. Parallel validation also revealed a fixture problem: the default Next dev server could delete the browser gate's nested `.next` config/build. Owned per-run build/config paths now live outside that shared directory, are ignored by Git and cleaned up; the complete real-browser gate and five layout tests pass while their servers overlap. This is not a claim to have fixed all previously observed HTTP/browser harness flakiness.

Current targeted gates pass 2,121 tests: 301 issuer, 187 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 393 quick-actions and 491 frontend unit/API tests; 50 identity, 80 license and 71 issuer/client-authority/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 22 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. Both Compose configurations validate with disposable values; YAML parsing and `git diff --check` pass. The frontend-wide type check still fails with exactly the same 17 unrelated baseline diagnostics. A clean complete frontend build, full-repository test pass, container build/rollout, production migration or complete Entra/vendor parity is not claimed. The overall platform goal remains open.

## Twenty-First Implemented Increment: Mandatory User and Group Application Access

### Registration Is Not Permission to Sign In

Every human OAuth authorization-code/device sign-in now requires an explicit, unexpired assignment of its actual canonical user or one of its direct canonical groups to that registered client. This applies to administrators as well; there is no implicit global-role exception, all-users import or optional compatibility bypass. Workload `client_credentials` remains a distinct application actor governed by its client/scope grants, not a guessed human/group identity. SAML, legacy directory/Kerberos and unrelated prototype authentication paths are **not** claimed to implement this new OAuth policy.

The user/group sign-in distinction is inspired by [Microsoft's application access-management concepts](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/enterprise-apps/what-is-access-management). This mandatory-only local policy is not Microsoft API compatibility, consent policy, tenant isolation or a claim of identical product/licensing behavior.

An assignment grants the ability to authenticate to the application, **not** a platform role, app role, OAuth permission consent, local/vendor license, vendor account or external provisioning success. Existing user/scope delegation checks and client enablement/generation still apply. The local license authority continues to enforce its own canonical roles, independent review, recipient eligibility and capacity. A test allocates a real local seat through an assigned administrative application, withdraws that operator's application lease and denies further writes through both gateway and direct policy without disabling the client or another assigned user.

### Separate Access Administration

- `application-access-reader` receives `applications.assignments.read` only. It can inspect bounded assignment state for a known client, not list directory profiles, read client secrets or edit registrations.
- `application-access-administrator` receives assignment read/manage, not client metadata/credential/registration management or license privileges. Conversely, `application-administrator` cannot assign application access merely by owning registration/credential permissions.
- Global canonical human administrators can manage protected-scope applications; delegated access staff cannot take over a protected client through its assignment API. The current registration policy is rechecked under the canonical client lock before writes. Workload actor labels, body-supplied roles/actors and an `oauth.admin` scope alone do not become access-management authority.
- Direct canonical self-grant/self-revocation is denied. A group grant/revocation is also denied when the operator is a current canonical member. Neither an OAuth subject alias nor a credential-service group claim can substitute for those canonical checks.

The self-beneficiary check is a fresh check at the write boundary, **not** complete long-term separation of duties. A separately authorized group administrator can change later group membership; combined duties, independent group-change approval and continuous access reviews still need policy. Canonical target checks and OAuth writes are not one distributed database transaction. A concurrently disabled/deleted target may leave an assigned historical lease, but fresh sign-in checks cannot turn that stale target into an eligible identity.

### Immutable, Bounded and Audited Entitlements

`/api/clients/:id/assignments` implements read/grant and `DELETE /api/clients/:id/assignments/:assignmentId` implements one-way withdrawal. Requests accept only an explicit UUID `requestId`, `targetType` (`user`/`group`), canonical `targetId`, 60–2,592,000 seconds and a 10–500 character reason. Target eligibility and current self/group membership come from the persistent identity authority. Disabled/unknown users, nonexistent groups, arbitrary expiration/approval/actor fields and selector aliases cannot create a grant. Disabled registered applications may be prepared with assignments, but remain unable to issue tokens until independently enabled. Workload-only registrations do not pretend to support human assignments.

Grant origin, canonical target, reason, source revision, duration and deadline are immutable. A live lease for the same client/type/target cannot be silently extended or duplicated with another UUID. Reusing the same request UUID acknowledges only the same canonical actor/client/target/reason/duration while its original grant remains active; it cannot resurrect withdrawn/expired evidence or retarget the request. A renewed lease needs a new reviewed grant; replacing a still-active lease first requires explicit withdrawal. Distinct direct/group sources may overlap.

Client-row locking serializes assignment writes against each other and client metadata/deletion. SQL triggers capture verified canonical actor provenance and atomic application-access audit; a failed audit rolls back both grant and revocation. SQL guards prohibit destructive history edits, actor/target/deadline rewrites and reactivation. Read responses include the actual client name, protected/manage flags, source target revisions and at most 100 current/history rows with truncation, not fabricated access or a complete directory report. Audit contains governance/personal identifiers and needs retention/off-host protection; a database-owner/superuser can still bypass application database controls.

### Live Membership and Application Contexts

Identity migration 009 gives each flat membership a server-generated immutable `membership_id`. Removing/readding the same user/group creates another incarnation; reapplying the migration preserves existing incarnations. The read-only, repeatable-read identity authorization snapshot now has schema 3 and returns bounded sorted `{ groupId, membershipId }` pairs alongside the existing canonical identity/role context. It contains no profile or credential. Role-only schema 2 cannot be guessed into an empty authoritative membership list. The issuer bounds this response to 64 KiB and 256 memberships; unsupported/excessive/corrupt source state fails closed, not partially authorized. These are explicit current limits, not directory-scale paging support.

A second narrowly scoped signed workload read at `/internal/identity/application-target` validates a known user/group plus the selected canonical operator. Its distinct purpose, exact signed actor/target tuple, fixed path/method/audience and 30-second lifetime prevent borrowing the existing subject-read proof for another target or an ordinary API. Neither endpoint uses issuer token-state introspection, preserving the earlier cycle-free issuer → identity authority design. Protected transport across untrusted networks is still required.

Human access/refresh and pending authorization/device checkpoints carry an `application_authorization` fingerprint of the actual client/user and all effective immutable assignment IDs plus relevant membership incarnations. Every integrated live check recomputes this from real sources, not JWT `groups`, submitted groups or credential-service group names. Withdrawal/expiry, membership removal/readdition or a new lease cannot revive old checkpoints, refresh or access. Fresh-process tests verify persisted behavior. General `groups` claims and legacy provider group mapping are not consolidated by this increment; those claims are deliberately not this gate's authorization source.

Overlapping assignments preserve a path for a **new** sign-in, but changing an effective source fences the former context even if another source remains. Unrelated users' grants do not advance the entire OAuth client generation or sign out every user. Context reads, identity snapshots and downstream effects are not globally atomic: a change after a check cannot recall an already accepted operation. Signature-only/legacy receivers and external federated sessions still require enforcement/termination work.

Disabling/resetting a directory account blocks sign-in and fences old sessions, but does not automatically erase every unexpired app assignment. A later re-enabled, newly authenticated identity may still use an unexpired retained lease. Withdraw the assignment when the entitlement itself must end. User deletion/group tombstones and membership incarnations prevent ID reuse/readdition from resurrecting old token contexts. No app assignment consumes or releases a license seat; local offboarding and real provider fulfillment remain separate workflows.

### Real Console and Reviewed Bootstrap

The OAuth/integrations console now exposes capability-based user/group assignment read, confirmed grant/revoke, explicit duration/reason and current/history state. Access-only staff can enter a known client UUID without acquiring profile-list or registration/secret permissions. Self controls are withheld where locally knowable; server checks remain mandatory, including group self-benefit. Malformed/uncertain receipts cannot become success, automatically repeat a mutation or silently change the selected client. A displayed running lease is not confirmation that the target can currently authenticate or that a vendor has provisioned anything.

This is a breaking security upgrade. Apply identity migration 009 before the new issuer/OAuth migration 008; retain earlier explicit issuer/subject links, canonical roles, protected signing files and client registrations. Existing users/clients are not automatically mass-assigned. Old human tokens/refresh/checkpoints lack the new context and require a new eligible sign-in. Plan maintenance and reviewed initial access **before** enforcing this in a real deployment; no production upgrade is performed here.

For the initial/recovery operator, `scripts/bootstrap-application-access.js` requires an explicit `OAUTH_BOOTSTRAP_DATABASE_URL`, `OAUTH_ISSUER`, `OAUTH_IDENTITY_AUTHORITY_URL` and a protected `OAUTH_SIGNING_KEY_FILE` accepted by the identity verifier. It obtains real signed target eligibility and writes an audited canonical user lease for only 60–28,800 seconds. No permanent/group/bootstrap-all option or runtime service-token shortcut exists. It does not create credentials, identity links, admin roles or client registrations. Identity role bootstrap from increment seventeen is a separate, reviewed operation when needed.

From the repository root, with those secrets/URLs already provisioned securely:

```sh
node services/core/oauth-provider/scripts/bootstrap-application-access.js \
  "$BROWSER_AUTH_CLIENT_ID" "$CANONICAL_ADMIN_USER_ID" 3600 \
  "Reviewed initial administrator application access"
```

Use the actual configured client ID (commonly `web-app`) and an existing enabled canonical UUID, not a username/OAuth subject guess. Establish an independently authorized second administrator and reviewed regular entitlements before emergency access expires; the runtime self-administration guard is not bypassed by being the first administrator. The Dockerfile packages this helper but no container build/rollout is claimed. Explicit development-only protocol demo bypass remains nonproduction and is not an alternative persistent assignment authority.

### Evidence and Remaining Scope

PostgreSQL tests prove immutable membership incarnations and a repeatable snapshot during a concurrent membership removal; application tests prove responsibility separation, target/actor-bound retry, concurrent lease serialization, expiry/regrant, direct/group overlap, atomic audit rollback, source loss, history guards and migration reapplication. Owned accelerated SQL-expiry fixtures temporarily disable their own schema's guard/audit triggers, restore them before authorization checks and never weaken production API lifetime validation. Actual issuer/gateway/direct identity/policy/quick-actions tests cover canonical group removal/readd, forged credential group claims, selective license-write denial, refresh/source-outage rollback and pending code/approved device nonresurrection. An actual separate CLI process verifies protected, bounded bootstrap without minting administrative roles.

Actual Chrome → Next → issuer/identity/PostgreSQL tests use distinct registration/access/user actors, a public S256 app and an owned callback fixture. They deny unassigned sign-in, grant/revoke through the real console, reject old refresh/access, then grant group access and prove removal/readdition cannot revive the old token. Read-only staff and canonical self-denial require no directory-profile/registration lists. The callback/credential service are isolated fixtures, not deployed vendor/MFA/password infrastructure. Screenshots are inspected in owned disposable contexts; no live user account, vendor or production database is changed.

Independent consent/assignment approval and reviews, app roles and application ownership, nested/dynamic groups, tenant/resource scope, canonical general group claims, complete inventory pagination, workload permission/credential policy, verified MFA and legacy receiver/SAML/provider consolidation remain open. Mandatory OAuth sign-in assignment is not complete Entra application governance or Microsoft 365 licensing.

Current targeted gates pass 2,193 tests: 334 issuer, 189 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 393 quick-actions and 505 frontend unit/API tests; 53 identity, 80 license and 89 issuer/assignment/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 24 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. Both Compose variants validate with disposable configuration values; YAML parsing, JavaScript syntax checks and `git diff --check` pass. The frontend-wide type check still fails with exactly the same 17 unrelated diagnostics. Owned processes, SQL clusters and per-run build/config files are cleaned up. A full-repository green build, Docker image build/deployment, live production migration or real vendor integration is not claimed. The original platform goal remains open.

## Twenty-Second Implemented Increment: Verified MFA and Factor-Backed PIM

Human OAuth authentication now has a persistent, issuer-owned TOTP authority, keyed by the canonical user UUID. A credential provider's `mfaEnabled` flag, claimed `amr`, successful password response, an arbitrary submitted code or pending enrollment cannot establish this proof. The existing credential provider remains the first-factor authority; its optional upstream MFA is separate and is not silently bypassed or promoted to canonical assurance.

### Own-account enrollment and real authentication evidence

- The real `/mfa` console replaces sample users, invented aggregate status and nonfunctional global policy switches. `GET /api/mfa` reports only the verified bearer owner's factor status. Workload actors, caller-selected users, query aliases and unknown mutation fields are rejected; no directory-list or global-administrator privilege is needed for this self-service.
- `POST /api/mfa/enroll` rechecks the actual username/password, any required upstream MFA, the same credential subject and the same live canonical identity context. It generates a unique 160-bit secret, returns the manual authenticator configuration once, and stores only encrypted pending material. Setup expires after ten minutes and is bound to that exact persisted OAuth session ID. Starting another setup requires another successful password check and replaces the pending setup, not an active factor.
- `POST /api/mfa/confirm` requires an actual six-digit TOTP from that session's pending secret. Confirmation atomically enables the factor, advances its persistent incarnation and audits the change. The old session does not gain MFA; all its previous access/refresh and pending code/device contexts are fenced. The owner must sign in again using password and an unused OpenDirectory code.
- Enabled factors are mandatory for both authorization-code login and device approval. The issuer alone creates `amr: ["pwd","otp"]` and the exact versioned `mfa_authorization` context from the verified database result. An unenrolled account receives only `amr: ["pwd"]`. The two login fields deliberately distinguish the password provider's `mfaCode` from the canonical issuer's `otpCode`; both are needed if both independent systems require a factor.
- `POST /api/mfa/disable` requires the same independently reverified credentials and an unused current factor. It advances rather than deletes the incarnation and invalidates existing contexts, including strong sessions. Disabling/re-enrolling cannot resurrect older weak or strong tokens. There is no administrator HTTP bypass, sample secret, generated recovery-code claim or memory fallback after a storage error.

The authenticator uses SHA-1, six digits and 30-second steps with a bounded adjacent-step window. The algorithm and published vectors follow [RFC 6238](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc6238.html); the method names follow [RFC 8176](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8176.html). A row lock serializes verification across issuer instances. Accepted counters cannot be used again, including for setup confirmation, login, device approval and disablement. Five failed verifications persist a five-minute lock across instances; replacing a locked pending setup cannot reset it. There is no automatic code retry. Wait for a new authenticator code after a successful use.

### Live assurance and actual privileged activation

Access checks bind the exact methods, original authentication time, canonical user and persistent factor revision. A current read-only source lookup at issuer access-state/userinfo and the integrated receivers rejects changed or missing assurance. Authorization-code/device redemption rechecks the snapshot; refresh retains rather than invents or renews the original proof. Source/key failures deny access and refresh instead of downgrading to password-only authentication. A failed refresh transaction does not consume its credential. Bounded PostgreSQL statement/lock timeouts also cancel stalled source queries server-side; reads do not write or lock factor rows.

Canonical PIM activation now requires the owner's verified password-plus-TOTP evidence from a login at most five minutes old, in addition to the existing eligibility, independent review and live requester/reviewer checks. A fresh password alone returns `428 verified_mfa_required`; an old authentication still requires a fresh login. A refresh cannot reset this clock. Approval does not create a role, and enrollment does not activate PIM. Successful activation still changes the real canonical role authority, invalidates the owner's old role context and requires another authenticated sign-in before use. Actual license/orchestration receivers continue checking that same authority.

Evidence and factor transitions are committed transactionally. Audit failure rolls back enablement, disablement and successful counter consumption. Audit rows contain canonical user/session/action/revision, not seeds, passwords or codes, and cannot be updated/deleted through the supported database model. Factor incarnations cannot be deleted/rewound, and an active seed cannot be replaced in place. This is not off-host immutable audit storage; the trusted database owner remains outside this runtime authorization boundary.

### Breaking rollout and key custody

OAuth migration `009_verified_mfa.sql`, the new shared MFA contract in both issuer and identity images, a reviewed persistent encryption key and user reauthentication are required together. Older human tokens lack the mandatory assurance context and are rejected; workload tokens do not acquire human MFA. Existing users are not automatically enrolled. They may still use explicitly assigned normal applications with password authentication, but cannot activate PIM until they configure and use the canonical factor. Previously configured upstream MFA is not migrated or falsely claimed as this factor.

Generate a distinct encryption key as the actual OAuth service file owner, using an existing private `0700` directory:

```sh
node services/core/oauth-provider/scripts/mfa-key.js /absolute/private/directory/mfa-encryption.key
```

The command uses exclusive creation, private `0600` permissions and durable writes; it prints only a public key identifier and never overwrites an existing file. Configure the absolute `OAUTH_MFA_KEY_FILE` for a native issuer. Both Compose variants mount `OAUTH_MFA_KEY_HOST_FILE` read-only at `/run/opendirectory/mfa-encryption.key`, reject automatic host-path creation, and set the runtime variable explicitly. Match the file owner to the configured OAuth service UID/GID, as for the signing key; do not make the key world-readable. This check does not generate or install production secrets.

Pending/active seeds use AES-256-GCM with authenticated canonical-user and pending/active-purpose binding, random nonces and an explicit key identifier. All replicas must share the same protected key and database. Missing, invalid, wrong-owner or publicly readable key files fail startup; a different key cannot decrypt existing factors or validate their existing assurance. Never replace this key as a substitute for rotation or account recovery. Back up the protected key independently of encrypted database backups and test restoration under the service owner. Automated key-ring rotation, KMS/HSM custody and reviewed lost-factor recovery are not implemented. Explicit development demo mode remains forbidden in production; ordinary persistence failure never selects it.

### Evidence and remaining scope

Tests cover published TOTP vectors, strict code parsing, authenticated encryption/purpose binding, session-bound and expiring setup, failed-attempt persistence, independent/concurrent verifiers, immutable evidence, audit rollback, nonresurrection, migration reapplication, wrong keys and real source outages. Actual HTTP tests exercise password rechecks, owner isolation, access/refresh/pending-code/device fencing, method-preserving refresh, independent issuer restart, real device proof and bounded SQL cancellation. Actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/PostgreSQL testing configures the factor through the own-account UI, removes its secret after confirmation, denies the weaker token, signs in with a real code, and performs independently reviewed PIM activation and revocation. This does not use the legacy global MFA map as proof.

Current targeted gates pass 2,237 tests: 352 issuer, 189 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 393 quick-actions and 517 frontend unit/API tests; 53 identity, 80 license and 103 issuer/assignment/MFA/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 34 browser-mediation core, 24 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. Both Compose variants validate with disposable values and the explicit read-only MFA mount. JavaScript syntax and `git diff --check` pass. The frontend type check has exactly the same 17 unrelated diagnostics, with no additions. The real MFA completion screen is visually checked without exposing its removed seed. Owned listeners, SQL clusters and isolated browser build/config files are cleaned up.

No container build, production key generation/migration, deployment or vendor fulfillment is performed. This increment is not phishing-resistant MFA, passkeys/WebAuthn, SMS, recovery codes, an assurance-level certification, global enrollment enforcement, tenant/resource conditional access or mandatory-PIM policy for every direct role grant. Existing authorized direct role grants remain a separate path. Remaining legacy credential/MFA/SAML/device/provisioning receivers still require consolidation; an already accepted remote effect is not recalled by a later source change. The wider Entra-like platform objective remains open.

## Twenty-Third Implemented Increment: Purpose-Limited Owner MFA Recovery

Canonical MFA now supports owner-held, single-use recovery secrets without turning recovery into an administrator reset, password-only sign-in or privileged temporary access pass. The design uses separately saved recovery codes rather than security questions or an administrative disable flag, patterns discussed in [OWASP's MFA recovery guidance](https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Multifactor_Authentication_Cheat_Sheet.html#resetting-mfa) and [NIST's account-recovery guidance](https://pages.nist.gov/800-63-4/sp800-63b.html). This implementation is not an assurance-level certification or a claim to implement all requirements in those documents.

### Explicit owner provisioning, not a prototype backup inventory

After normal MFA setup and a fresh sign-in, the own-account MFA console can explicitly create/replace eight random 128-bit recovery codes. `POST /api/mfa/recovery-codes` requires the live human bearer, independently reverified same-user password/provider MFA, and a separate unused current OpenDirectory TOTP. No role grants, global administrator privilege, user-list capability or caller-selected beneficiary are involved. A different username, query selector or extra identity field is rejected; an administrator bearer does not substitute for the target's factors.

The plaintext codes are returned only in that successful write response. The console validates the complete eight-code receipt, shows them only in component memory and lets the owner discard the display. It does not write codes, passwords, seeds or recovery challenges to local/session storage, cookies or URLs. `GET /api/mfa/recovery-codes` exposes only a verified remaining count, never existing code values. Older enabled factors start with zero recovery codes; no usable codes are guessed, automatically migrated or silently generated for existing users. Owners must deliberately generate and safely save them while they still have their authenticator.

Only SHA-256 hashes with a distinct recovery purpose and canonical-user binding are persisted. Each code is bound to the current factor incarnation and is valid only until consumed, replaced or that incarnation changes. Replacing codes revokes unused predecessors and pending recovery setups transactionally. Rotation itself leaves the already verified factor and its legitimate sessions intact; it is not a new login. A final live actor check protects plaintext disclosure if authorization changes while generation is in progress. An uncertain/lost receipt never produces a success banner or automatic rotation retry.

### Recovery is registration-only and never creates an application session

The public `/auth/mfa-recovery` screen is reachable from the normal login page and does not bootstrap a browser session. Its two exact native Next POST handlers (`/api/auth/oidc/recovery/start` and `/api/auth/oidc/recovery/confirm`) deliberately resolve before generic legacy-auth rewrites. A dynamic route in this position would be captured by those rewrites; actual browser testing caught that routing error and verifies the fixed handlers. The token mediator enforces the configured same origin, a non-simple explicit intent header, JSON-only bounded input, fixed action/body allowlists, no query selectors, fixed internal issuer origin, no redirects, bounded response/time limits and `no-store`. It forwards neither browser cookies/bearers nor the confidential application's secret.

1. `POST /oauth/mfa/recovery/start` first verifies the real username/password and any MFA independently required by the password provider. The issuer resolves the live canonical identity itself. A valid unused saved code is then consumed under the same factor-row transaction that creates a new pending authenticator and immutable recovery audit. The existing factor remains enabled; code consumption cannot downgrade the account to password-only authentication.
2. The response contains only the new authenticator configuration and a purpose-limited, ten-minute opaque setup challenge. Its 256-bit secret is stored only as a hash. The pending seed is encrypted using the existing protected MFA key and authenticated canonical-user plus exact recovery-ticket purpose. The challenge is not a JWT, API bearer, OAuth authorization/device grant, refresh credential, app assignment, administrator permission or PIM proof.
3. `POST /oauth/mfa/recovery/confirm` needs that exact unexpired, unconsumed challenge and an actual code from the new authenticator. It rechecks the saved issuer/subject and exact canonical user/link/role generations. Disable/delete/unlink/reset, a role change, expiry, supersession, proof mismatch or unavailable authority cannot be bypassed by the saved challenge. Re-enabling the user does not revive it.
4. Successful confirmation atomically consumes the challenge, replaces rather than disables the active authenticator, advances the factor incarnation, records its accepted counter and commits both recovery and factor audit. The database permits this otherwise-forbidden active-seed change only with that transaction's actually consumed, matching-incarnation recovery witness. An unconsumed or older witness cannot authorize it.
5. All old factor-bound access/refresh and pending code/device snapshots lose live authority, including previously MFA-authenticated sessions. Remaining old backup codes also become unusable through their incarnation binding. The completion response issues no session/cookie/token. The owner must perform the normal assigned-application login with password plus a new code, and deliberately generate/save a new backup family afterward. Recovery does not create, extend or approve any directory role, PIM lease or license allocation.

Starting with a different valid backup supersedes any outstanding setup for that owner. Replaying one backup or one completion challenge cannot win twice across independent issuer instances. Failed backup guesses and failed new-factor codes have persistent five-attempt/five-minute throttling; ordinary password-provider failures do not consume a valid backup. Audit/storage/source failure does not silently succeed or select a demo map. A failed start transaction preserves the backup; a failed completion transaction preserves the old factor and unconsumed challenge. If the write committed but its response or final disclosure check was lost, the result remains explicitly uncertain: use a different backup only for an explicitly new start, or test normal login with the already configured new authenticator after an uncertain completion. Never automatically resubmit a spent credential.

### Operational boundary and verification

OAuth migration `010_mfa_recovery.sql` and the coordinated issuer/web-app update are required. The existing protected MFA encryption key remains mandatory; this feature is not automatic encryption-key rotation, a plaintext-secret export or a database-recovery mechanism. Ledger origin/hash/expiry fields and consumed/revoked state are immutable/one-way. Audit failure rolls back code rotation/consumption, ticket issuance, factor replacement and successful counter changes. Database owners remain outside the runtime trust boundary; this is not an off-host tamper-proof archive.

The expanded real workload exposed a separate availability defect: signed internal checkpoints shared the directory's public 1,000-per-15-minute IP bucket. A reproduced run recorded 1,002 directory requests and two rate denials, preventing refresh even after the deliberately blocked SQL source recovered. The two exact signed authority POST routes now have their own bounded pre-verification ingress budget (6,000/minute per peer IP) and verifier-derived issuer budget (5,000/minute, shared across both purposes); only the existing exact audience/type/purpose/signature/body-bound proof can reach their SQL source. The normal public directory bucket is unchanged. Anonymous/forged requests remain bounded and cannot select that identity or access a resource. These in-process admission counters are not distributed tenant quotas or a DDoS/HA guarantee.

Verification includes strict hash/challenge parsing, real PostgreSQL concurrency, replay, expiry, source/role-generation fencing, one-way evidence, migration reapplication and audit rollback. Actual issuer/identity/gateway/policy/orchestration tests deny old strong access/refresh and pending code/device grants after replacement, reject administrator/identity aliases and preserve ordinary users' inability to write licenses. A separate test disables an owner during generation and checks that the committed plaintext codes are not disclosed. Actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/PostgreSQL testing generates the backups through the own-account form, recovers in a completely signed-out browser, checks absence of cookies/bearer/storage leakage, confirms removal of the displayed new seed and requires a normal fresh MFA login with unchanged canonical roles. Recovery completion is visually checked without including its secret material.

Current targeted gates pass 2,293 tests: 368 issuer, 194 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 393 quick-actions and 533 frontend unit/API tests; 53 identity, 80 license and 118 issuer/application/MFA/recovery/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 37 browser-mediation core, 25 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. The admission tests cover public/source budget separation, shared bounded verified purposes, forged proofs and unavailable sources without changing public limits. A gateway negative-JWT test initially encountered a transient socket hang-up under parallel validation; the full 94-test suite and the isolated case subsequently pass without code or assertion changes. Both Compose variants validate offline with disposable values. JavaScript syntax and `git diff --check` pass. TypeScript retains exactly the same 17 unrelated diagnostics with no additions. Owned listeners/clusters and isolated browser build/config files are cleaned up; the MFA recovery completion screen is visually checked without exposing secrets.

Lost-all-factors/password recovery, independently reviewed helpdesk identity proofing, recovery-change notification delivery/outbox, passkeys/phishing-resistant assurance, mandatory tenant-wide enrollment/conditional policies, automatic key-ring rotation and legacy provider/SAML/MFA consolidation remain open. The password provider's separate authenticator cannot be bypassed with an OpenDirectory backup. These codes do not provision Microsoft licenses or supply a temporary privileged access pass. No production key generation, migration, deployment, container image build or vendor fulfillment is performed. The wider Entra-like platform objective remains open.

## Twenty-Fourth Implemented Increment: Application MFA and Authentication-Age Policies

OAuth applications now have a persistent minimum authentication policy: require canonical password-plus-TOTP authentication, with an original login age between five minutes and one day. This connects the verified factor authority to actual application and API admission instead of advertising a global MFA switch. Authentication-age policy is conceptually related to [Entra sign-in frequency](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/conditional-access/concept-session-lifetime); the proof uses the issuer's original `auth_time`, described by [OpenID Connect Core](https://www.openid.net/specs/openid-connect-core-1_0.html). This is a custom server-side application rule, not complete OIDC request-`max_age`, authentication-context/challenge interoperability or Entra Conditional Access conformance.

### Separate, freshly authenticated policy authority

- `application-authentication-reader` reads a known application's minimal policy without directory/profile, credential or registration-inventory permissions. `application-authentication-administrator` additionally changes nonprivileged applications' policy. The latter is a protected canonical role: ordinary role administrators cannot delegate it. Global administration is required for current privileged/custom/role-bearing scopes. Classification follows actual scopes, not an application's friendly name.
- `GET /api/clients/:id/authentication` returns only the application ID/name/version, required flag, maximum age and actual protection/editability/fresh-login capability. Normal registration DTOs do not expose these policy fields or confer the separate policy-read capability. Metadata editing, secret rotation and application-access assignment permissions do not confer policy-write authority.
- `PUT` requires the exact `If-Match` client version, a boolean requirement, an integer maximum age of 300–86,400 seconds and a reviewed reason of 10–500 characters. The caller must be a live canonical human with a genuinely bound password-plus-TOTP proof from no more than five minutes ago. A claimed `amr`, provider flag, fresh refresh `iat`, ordinary password login, workload token or submitted identity selector cannot substitute for it.
- Even a globally authorized operator cannot change the policy of the client that issued their current bearer. Use an independently assigned management client. This is client/duty separation, not a claim that two different humans must approve every policy write. Normal metadata writes cannot set/reset the MFA fields; they preserve the locked current policy, including across other client changes.
- The row-locked conditional write, client-generation advance, reason and policy snapshot are audited transactionally. Audit failure cannot acknowledge a committed configuration. Both enforcement and later relaxation advance the same durable client generation, so restoring a weaker setting cannot revive old human/workload tokens, refresh credentials or pending authorization/device checkpoints. A source outage does not select an older positive process-local policy.

The actual OAuth console has a separate known-ID policy form and a registration-card shortcut for authorized readers. It shows only verified policy state, hides writes without both the independent capability and server-confirmed editability, requests a fresh login where needed, and requires explicit confirmation before changing generations. Stale/partial/lost/mismatched receipts cannot become success or automatic retry. Changing the selected client or losing management permission clears stale confirmation. The selected-client notice identifies the original target if another operation finishes later.

### Runtime enforcement, not a refresh-time MFA upgrade

Human authorization-code login and device approval cannot complete password-only authentication when the selected current client requires MFA. The issuer already verifies the real canonical factor and now checks the client rule before creating its login checkpoint. Code/device redemption checks it again before creating a session. Every integrated access-state check evaluates the current client rule and original bound factor proof, including the core gateway, direct identity/policy APIs and per-step delegated orchestration. Real license endpoints therefore reject an affected old or too-old bearer before its existing role could be used; this rule does not allocate seats, create license permissions or recall an already accepted remote write.

Refresh retains the original `auth_time`, methods and canonical factor context. It cannot reset the policy's clock, replace an absent second factor, re-expand removed roles or update an old client generation. A genuinely signed historical MFA session with an unexpired JWT but authentication older than the configured policy is denied at actual receivers and cannot refresh; its refresh credential is not consumed on that denial. Recent genuine MFA for the same client still works. PIM retains its independent, stricter five-minute activation requirement even if the application rule is disabled or allows a longer age.

Workload `client_credentials` authentication does not acquire or pretend to satisfy human MFA. A newly authenticated permitted workload remains possible without a TOTP. A policy write still advances the shared client generation, so previous workload tokens also become inactive and need normal new client authentication. This does not implement workload credential/consent policy, tenant/resource audiences or machine impersonation of human roles.

### First binding without a temporary privileged session

An application may require MFA before its newly assigned user has any factor. The public `/auth/mfa-enroll` screen solves that bootstrap without an opt-out application session, administrator bypass, recovery-code substitution or fabricated provisional user token:

1. The two exact native Next POST handlers at `/api/auth/oidc/enrollment/start` and `/confirm` use the same fixed-origin, bounded, `no-store` token-mediator transport as recovery, but have their own fixed purpose, field allowlist and non-simple enrollment intent header. They forward neither browser cookies/bearers nor the confidential client's secret. They resolve before legacy auth rewrites; no dynamic selector determines the upstream action.
2. `POST /oauth/mfa/enrollment/start` verifies the real username/password and any independently required upstream MFA. The issuer itself resolves the subject to an active canonical user. The named client must be enabled, support a human flow and actually require MFA; the real current canonical direct/group application assignment must exist. Caller-supplied subjects, user IDs, scopes, methods or administrative flags are not enrollment selectors.
3. Only an account without an active canonical factor can start. A configured factor cannot be replaced with password-only registration; use its ordinary authenticated management or the separately scoped saved-code recovery flow. First binding inherits the credential provider's assurance and is not independent identity proofing or phishing-resistant bootstrap.
4. The issuer returns only a ten-minute registration challenge and the new authenticator configuration. The 256-bit challenge secret is purpose-hashed; the pending seed is encrypted/authenticated for that exact enrollment ID and canonical user. Its pseudo-owner identifier is not an OAuth session, and its pending ciphertext cannot be confirmed through the normal bearer-session setup or recovery purpose.
5. Confirmation requires the actual new authenticator code, an unexpired/unconsumed/unsuperseded challenge, the same original factor incarnation, the exact current identity/link/role context, the same client generation/policy and the same effective application-access fingerprint. Actual canonical group removal/readdition invalidates a saved enrollment even when a direct overlapping lease still allows a new enrollment. Client-policy withdrawal/re-enforcement or a directory generation change cannot revive old setup evidence.
6. Successful confirmation consumes the ticket and atomically enables/audits the factor in the existing MFA authority. Weaker human token contexts become inactive through its incarnation advance. No session/cookie/access/refresh token, role, PIM eligibility or license is issued. The user must then perform the normal application login with password and a new, unused code, and explicitly generate/save recovery codes afterward.

Concurrent confirmations cannot win twice. Wrong-code throttling persists across instances and cannot be cleared by restarting a locked setup. Source/audit failures roll back mutations rather than reporting success; a committed start whose response/final source check was lost remains explicitly uncertain. Completion audit failure does not enable the factor or spend the ticket. Enrollment origin/expiry and consumed evidence cannot be rewritten or extended through the supported SQL model. These are trusted-database-owner boundaries, not off-host immutable evidence.

### Breaking rollout and scope

Apply OAuth migration `011_application_authentication.sql` and coordinate issuer, identity role-contract and web-app images. The existing protected persistent MFA encryption key remains required; no new plaintext-secret fallback or production key provisioning is added. Both exact enrollment routes and the separate policy capabilities must ship with enforcement so that newly assigned users are not trapped behind a required factor they cannot register.

Existing and newly created applications retain an explicit nonrequired policy until an authorized operator configures it. New registrations still start disabled with no automatic human assignment. This is a reviewed opt-in application-policy increment, not automatic organization-wide security defaults. A safe rollout is: create the disabled registration; have the independently authenticated policy authority configure the required MFA/age; assign eligible users/groups with the separate access duty; reload the current client version before conditional activation; then let users register only their factor and perform normal sign-in. Disabling the application minimum neither disables an already configured personal factor nor weakens PIM. Review every privileged issuing application: a rule on one client is not a resource-/role-/tenant-wide requirement on tokens from all other approved clients.

Verification covers strict policy data, fresh canonical management proof, exact versions, immutable actor audit, source/membership/client-generation fencing, code anti-replay, SQL rollback and migration reapplication. Actual HTTP tests exercise weaker and genuinely historical signed MFA sessions, live denial at five receiving surfaces, unchanged refresh authentication time, independent issuer restart, pending code/device nonresurrection and workload separation. Actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/PostgreSQL testing uses different metadata, assignment and authentication duties; confirms that weak management is read-only; enforces the rule through the real console; denies password-only app access; performs assigned public first binding without a session/storage leak; and finally obtains a genuine MFA-backed normal app token with no new roles. The separate policy reader cannot mutate it. The completion screen is visually checked without including its removed seed.

Current targeted gates pass 2,343 tests: 383 issuer, 194 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 adapter, 393 quick-actions and 552 frontend unit/API tests; 53 identity, 80 license and 131 issuer/application/MFA/recovery/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 39 browser-mediation core, 26 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests. Both Compose variants validate offline with disposable values. JavaScript syntax and `git diff --check` pass. TypeScript retains exactly the same 17 unrelated diagnostics with no additions. Owned test listeners/clusters and isolated browser build/config files are cleaned up; no production migration or deployment is performed.

Tenant/resource and role-wide assurance requirements, default enforcement across all privileged applications, device/risk/location conditions, independent preverified initial identity proofing, complete standard reauthentication challenges, passkeys, lost-all-factors recovery/notification delivery and remaining legacy/federated-session receivers still need implementation. An offline signature-only relying party's own session is not continuously revoked by this issuer rule. Cross-database admission checks do not recall already accepted effects. Vendor-backed Microsoft license fulfillment is unchanged and remains open; no live key generation, production migration, deployment or container-image build is performed. The full platform objective remains open.

## Twenty-Fifth Implemented Increment: Client-Independent Role Authentication

Per-application policy alone leaves another approved client as a possible weaker authentication path for the same privileged human. The canonical directory now owns MFA and original-authentication-age requirements for every catalog role. This is a bounded role-based control, conceptually related to [Entra authentication strengths](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity/authentication/concept-authentication-strengths), not a complete Conditional Access engine, phishing-resistant method or interoperability claim.

### Canonical policy and separate duties

- `authentication-policy-reader` can read only a known role's minimal policy. The protected `authentication-policy-administrator` additionally manages nonprotected roles without directory profiles, role assignments, application credentials, PIM reviews or license allocation. Only a global administrator can change protected-role policies or the broad baseline `user` role. Ordinary role administrators cannot grant this protected security duty.
- `GET /api/identity/authentication/roles/:role` returns exactly role/version, requirement/age and protected/locked/editability/fresh-login flags. It does not list users, memberships or role holders. The independent role-console panel remains usable without role-assignment catalog/read permission.
- `PUT` requires the exact `If-Match: "od-role-authentication:<role>:<version>"`, exactly `required`, `maxAgeSeconds` and `reason`, an actual boolean, integer 300–86,400 seconds and reviewed 10–500-character reason. Only a canonical human with bound password-plus-TOTP proof from no more than five minutes ago can mutate it. Application-policy administration, registration editing, role assignment and provider-submitted MFA flags are not substitutes.
- Policy administration itself has an unchangeable MFA requirement with a five-minute original-login age. The database constraint, update guard, authoritative source validator and read-only UI enforce this minimum; even global API administration cannot relax it. This does not automatically require MFA for every existing administrative role.
- A row lock serializes conditional writes. After obtaining that lock, the transaction rereads the actor's current canonical user, link, live roles, role-policy fingerprint and authority. A role/security reset while a writer was actually waiting cannot commit using the earlier admission. Policy/version, verified actor, reason and before/after evidence commit together. Audit loss rolls back the configuration. SQL guards retain policies/evidence and prevent version reset, deletion or reediting the fixed minimum.

This is an explicit policy-administration authority, not a two-human approval workflow. A globally authorized actor can configure the rule for their own global role after fresh MFA; the change also invalidates their previous context and the console warns that normal reauthentication is required. It cannot repair, grandfather or mint a replacement session in the policy response.

### Enforcement across clients and scopes

The directory's response to signed authority requests is now schema 4 and contains a strict aggregate `{required,maxAgeSeconds}`. Every actually live canonical held role participates, including direct and activated PIM grants. The minimum age among required policies wins; client policy and PIM activation retain their independent requirements. `rolesRevision` includes sorted role grants and each participating durable policy version. Every policy change, including disabling a rule and later restoring it, therefore fences old contexts without worker timing or a process-local positive cache. Missing/malformed policy data, a missing role row, legacy schema 3 or an unavailable table cannot become an implicit nonrequired rule.

Login/device approval obtains the real factor proof before creating a checkpoint. Code/device redemption, refresh and integrated live receiver admission use current canonical policy. Omitting `roles` scope or selecting a non-MFA application does not remove the human's actual held roles from policy evaluation. Refresh retains original `auth_time` and cannot upgrade a password session, replace the factor incarnation or renew authentication age. A genuinely signed, unexpired historical MFA session is still denied when its original login is too old; rejected refresh does not consume its credential.

This checks the human's held roles, not only the role they claim to be exercising on one request. A restricted role holder must reauthenticate even for a lower-privilege client/scope while holding that role. This does not implement resource-specific step-up, dropping a selected active role, standard OIDC request `max_age` challenges or risk/device signals. Workload `client_credentials` remains outside human role/MFA policy and gains no fabricated second factor.

Actual local license allocation now denies a weaker alternate-client bearer under a required `license-manager` policy before touching seats, while a normal fresh two-factor login with the existing role can allocate through the real receiver. Policy does not grant that role, assign application access, create an entitlement or perform Microsoft Graph fulfillment. Already accepted remote effects and offline-only relying-party sessions are not recalled.

### First binding and breaking rollout

The existing signed-out, purpose-limited first-binding transport now accepts a live required application policy **or** the user's live required role policy. An enabled human-flow client and actual direct/group assignment remain mandatory. Existing factors cannot be password-replaced; this still verifies the password/provider, canonical subject, policy/client/identity/application incarnations, ten-minute ticket and unused new TOTP. It returns only factor completion, never an application token, role or license. Even a newly assigned policy administrator can therefore register their initial factor through a normally nonrequired assigned client before their first actual MFA login. Initial binding is not independent identity proofing or phishing-resistant enrollment.

Apply identity migration `010_role_authentication.sql` and coordinate directory, issuer and web images. Both service Dockerfiles now include the shared role-authentication contract. Schema 4 and the revised role fingerprint require **all existing human sessions to sign in again on this upgrade**; no schema-3 or stale-context fallback exists. Workloads are not silently converted into humans. Existing OAuth MFA migrations, protected persistent factor key and both exact browser enrollment routes remain prerequisites. No production migration, key generation, image build or deployment is performed here.

All other role policies are explicitly nonrequired until reviewed configuration, including existing administrative and license roles. Migration reapplication preserves configured policy/version and never resets it. Before enabling a sensitive role rule, independently provision the protected policy duty and real application assignments, verify the public first-binding path, configure the reviewed requirement/age and plan affected human reauthentication. Review every role conferring a sensitive permission: configuring `license-manager` alone does not require MFA for an `admin` who does not hold that role. The fixed security-duty minimum is not universal organization-wide security defaults. Mandatory PIM, tenant/resource isolation and complete receiving-service enforcement remain open.

Verification includes strict source/schema/receipt contracts; protected duty delegation; original proof/age, omitted scope and alternate-client negatives; SQL concurrency, audit rollback, immutable policy and migration evidence; and a real blocked writer denied after independent actor reset. Actual issuer/gateway/directory/policy/quick-actions tests exercise pending-grant nonresurrection, license seat nonmutation, normal strong allocation, original refresh time, historical signed age and role-source outage/recovery. Actual Chrome/Next/directory/issuer/PostgreSQL tests register the policy operator's first factor without a session, change the rule through the real console, invalidate weak browser refresh, register the affected assigned user through a nonrequired client and verify the fresh token's real MFA. A separate policy reader has no profile requests or mutation controls; its direct write is denied. The resulting read-only policy screen is visually checked without factor secrets. Credential verification in these integration tests is an isolated HTTP fixture, not deployed provider infrastructure.

Current targeted gates pass 2,394 tests: 391 issuer, 216 identity, 94 gateway, 361 policy, 32 license adapter, 393 quick-actions and 566 frontend unit/API tests; 57 identity, 80 license and 133 issuer/application/MFA/recovery/receiver PostgreSQL tests; 39 browser-mediation core, 27 actual Chrome/Next/issuer/identity/orchestration tests and five fixture-backed layout tests.
